# CFPB 4 attachment - CFPB 4-1 with DBFI Combined.pdf

> [!info] From project: [[_Project Info|DBFI and FIB non compliance]]

Company: First Interstate Bank
Product: Credit Card
Issue: Billing Error Investigation - Reliance on Unverified Contractual Terms
Complaint Narrative
This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s reliance on merchant-post-submitted terms that
merchant admits were initially omitted, that may not constitute a legally enforceable
agreement when denying a billing-error dispute. The issue presented is whether the issuer
relied on unsigned and previously undisclosed merchant terms - produced after the
transaction; without verifying whether those terms were ever clearly presented to or
unambiguously affirmatively agreed to by the consumer.
A creditor’s obligation under federal law is not merely to collect a merchant response,
but to conduct an independent and meaningful investigation of the billing-error claim
before determining whether the charge is valid.
This complaint concerns whether First Interstate Bank conducted the meaningful and
reasonable investigation required under the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z before
denying a billing-error dispute. The bank’s determination appears to rely on merchant-submitted
one-sided terms that were initially omitted, currently unsigned, and produced months after
the initial transaction and payment; which does not constitute a legally enforceable
agreement.
Under the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), when a creditor receives written
notice of a billing error, the creditor must investigate the matter and correct the error if
one exists. This statute governs credit-card billing disputes and establishes the
issuer’s duty to conduct a meaningful investigation rather than relying solely on
merchant assertions.
Regulation Z, which implements the Fair Credit Billing Act, requires that a creditor
conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim before denying a
billing error.
During the investigation of the disputed charge, the bank relied on merchant-submitted “terms
and agreement” documents as a primary basis for denying the billing error. However, there is no
evidence that those alleged terms were ever clearly presented to or affirmatively accepted by
the consumer at the time of the transaction.
There is also documented evidence provided by BOTH the merchant and the cardholder,
used and possessed by First Interstate Bank (FIB); showing that the merchant writes in text

roughly 1 month after the transaction, that the merchant omitted the terms and agreement at
time of purchase.
- This has been documented and verified through many channels, such as certified mail,
phone, fax, prior dated threads in writing, etc.
- This admission is critical because it confirms that the merchant’s alleged terms and
agreement were not presented to the consumer at the time of purchase. Without
clear notice of the terms and an unambiguous manifestation of assent, contract law
generally recognizes that no binding agreement to those terms exists.
Courts consistently hold that online contractual terms are enforceable only where consumers
receive reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and clearly manifest assent to those
terms.
If either element is missing - clear notice or unambiguous agreement - courts find that
no binding contract exists.
This principle has been repeatedly applied by federal courts evaluating website terms
and conditions, including, but not limited to Specht v. Netscape Communications
Corp., Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc., and Berman v. Freedom Financial Network,
LLC., etc.
In this matter, the merchant’s rebuttal relied on omitted, alleged terms created by the merchant
after payment to justify non-delivery of the original promised services that are uniform
across ads, marketing material, sales call transcript, website, merchant invoice, merchant
writing and documentation, etc.
However, there is no evidence that these omitted post-payment terms were clearly
presented to or unambiguously accepted & agreed to by the consumer during the
purchase process. (lack of mutual assent)
Screenshots from the merchant’s website also show that links to the merchant’s “Terms” and
“Privacy Policy” pages return 404 “Page Not Found” errors, further solidifying that terms were
inaccessible to consumers at the time of purchase.
Despite these issues, First Interstate Bank appears to have accepted the merchant’s
non-contractual defenses without independently evaluating whether a valid agreement
containing those terms was ever formed; while simultaneously ignoring cardholder’s evidence
and statements proving no mutually assenting, and/or signed agreement exists.
Merchant has deceptively created unsigned terms that are significantly and materially different
than the documented ads, sales, website, invoice, and material in writing uniformly affirm. This

bait and switch tactic is considered deception defined by the FTC, as well as being clearly
defined as deceptive acts and practices - that FIB is supporting and enforcing.
Where a merchant’s explanation relies on materially different terms initially omitted,
and introduced after the transaction; a reasonable investigation should evaluate whether
those terms were ever presented to or unambiguously agreed to by the consumer
before relying on them as the basis for denying a billing-error claim.
Where a creditor relies on merchant assertions without verifying whether those assertions are
legally valid or supported by the evidence submitted by the consumer, the investigation may
fail to satisfy the reasonable-investigation requirement imposed by the Fair Credit Billing
Act and Regulation Z.
Federal Compliance Framework
Federal law establishes clear obligations for credit-card issuers when a consumer disputes a
charge.
Under the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), when a creditor receives written notice of
a billing error, the creditor must investigate the matter and correct the error if one exists.
Regulation Z, which implements the Fair Credit Billing Act, further provides:
“The creditor must conduct a reasonable investigation of the consumer’s claim.”
12 CFR §1026.13(f)
Regulation Z also defines a billing error to include:
“A reflection on a statement of goods or services not accepted by the consumer or
not delivered as agreed.”
12 CFR §1026.13(a)(3)
The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau’s official interpretation clarifies that a creditor may
not deny a billing-error claim solely because a merchant asserts that the charge is valid.
Accordingly, a creditor must independently evaluate the evidence submitted by the
consumer and determine whether the goods or services were actually delivered as
agreed rather than simply accepting the merchant’s narrative.
Where a creditor relies on merchant representations or contractual defenses without
independently evaluating whether those defenses are legally valid, this raises concerns

regarding compliance with the investigation requirements of the Fair Credit Billing Act and
Regulation Z.
Failure to conduct a meaningful investigation may also raise concerns under the Consumer
Financial Protection Act (12 U.S.C. §5531), which prohibits unfair practices in consumer
financial services when consumers suffer substantial financial injury that they cannot
reasonably avoid.
Failure to comply with the investigation obligations imposed by the Fair Credit Billing Act may
also create liability under the Truth in Lending Act (15 U.S.C. §1640).
Issuer Accountability
Credit-card issuers are responsible for ensuring that billing-error determinations are based on a
reasonable investigation of the facts. Where a bank relies on merchant representations that
contradict evidence provided by the consumer, fails to verify the legal validity of merchant
“contractual” defenses, or denies a claim without independently evaluating whether services
were delivered as agreed, such conduct may violate the investigation obligations imposed by
the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
**These obligations exist specifically to prevent financial institutions from enforcing
charges that arise from misrepresentation, deception, or non-delivery of promised goods
or services.**
Investigation Concerns
In this dispute, the bank appears to have relied on merchant assertions regarding internal work
activity and alleged one-way, unsigned, post agreement terms - presented by the merchant
months after the chargeback and initial transaction date; rather than evaluating whether the
purchased service that the was advertised at the time of purchase was actually delivered as
agreed. (mutual assent)
The merchant and the issuer bear the burden of producing substantial evidence to prove that
the consumer unambiguously and affirmatively manifested assent to those terms, as the
courts and law require.

The disputed charge relates to a fixed-fee service program promising the acquisition, design,
furnishing, listing, and launch of an operational profiting Airbnb property. However, the promised
service outcome was never delivered.
Internal work activity claimed by the merchant does not satisfy delivery of the purchased
service where the transaction was marketed and sold as a fixed-fee program tied to a
specific outcome. (abundance of documented proof of evidence provided, including
issuer’s affirmed possession of this proof)
If the merchant’s omitted alleged post-transaction terms were never validly presented or
accepted unambiguously during the purchase process, reliance on those terms to justify denial
of the billing-error dispute are not legally binding.
Even if the merchant attempted to introduce new terms after the transaction, any modification of
the agreement would require clear notice of the new terms and unambiguous affirmative
acceptance by the consumer. The available record contains no evidence of such
unambiguous acceptance.
Partial Refund / “Downscale” Does Not Establish Contractual Agreement
The issuer’s determination appears to rely on the merchant’s decision to issue a partial
refund based on a unilateral “downscale” of the service from two properties to one
property after missed timelines and non-delivery of the original service scope clearly
communicated through documented text messages and emails to the team. (proof
provided and attached)
However, the merchant’s unilateral decision to partially refund the transaction or
reduce the scope of the service does not establish that the consumer
unambiguously agreed to new contractual terms governing the transaction.
A merchant’s statement that “no further refunds will be provided” does not
constitute evidence of mutual agreement. Contract law requires clear notice of
the proposed terms and an unambiguous manifestation of assent by the
consumer before those terms become binding.
A unilateral statement by the merchant regarding refund limitations or modified
service scope cannot, by itself, establish that the consumer accepted those
terms. Without clear evidence of affirmative agreement, such statements
remain unilateral conditions rather than mutually agreed contractual terms.
A reasonable investigation under Regulation Z should therefore
evaluate whether the consumer ever clearly agreed to the merchant’s
post-transaction refund conditions before relying on those conditions as
the basis for denying the billing-error dispute.

The merchant’s later decision to reduce the scope of the service after documented
communication on missed timelines and non-delivery of the original agreement;
does not change the fact that the service purchased and advertised at the time of
payment was never delivered as promised.
Absent evidence that the consumer affirmatively accepted the modified terms, the merchant’s
post-transaction conditions regarding refunds or scope reduction cannot reasonably be treated
as a binding contractual modification.
A reasonable investigation would therefore include evaluating whether:
● those terms were reasonably conspicuous to the consumer, AND
● the consumer unambiguously and affirmatively manifested assent to those terms
The available record raises concerns that the bank accepted the merchant’s contractual
defenses without verifying whether those legal prerequisites for contract formation were
satisfied.
The bank’s determination appears to treat the consumer’s continued communication with the
merchant during attempts to resolve the issue as acceptance of newly asserted contractual
terms. However, contract law requires a clear and unambiguous manifestation of assent
before contractual terms become binding. Continued communication or attempts to resolve a
dispute do not constitute affirmative agreement to new unsigned terms presented by the
merchant post-transaction.
Continued engagement with the merchant during attempts to resolve delays,
non-delivery, or missed timelines does not constitute authorization for billable hours or
acceptance of a new hourly service arrangement. The transaction was advertised and
sold as a fixed-fee service program promising the acquisition and launch of an
operational Airbnb property. There is no evidence that the consumer agreed to convert
the transaction into an hourly consulting engagement or authorized billable hours as a
substitute for delivery of the promised service outcome.
Accordingly, a reasonable investigation should evaluate whether the consumer ever
agreed to an hourly billing structure before relying on the merchant’s claimed internal
work activity as justification for denying the billing-error dispute.
Accordingly, the investigation must identify what evidence demonstrates that the consumer was
provided clear notice of the merchant’s omitted alleged terms and unambiguously manifested
assent to those terms at the time of initial purchase/payment, before they were relied upon to
deny the billing-error dispute.

Questions First Interstate Bank continues
to avoid, despite many documented
explicit requests for information:
The sequence of events raises three distinct issues that should be evaluated during a
reasonable investigation:
1. Whether the merchant’s omitted alleged contractual terms were clearly presented to the
consumer at the time of purchase.
2. Whether the consumer clearly and affirmatively, unambiguously agreed to those terms.
3. Whether the promised service outcome was actually delivered as advertised and agreed.
The available record raises concerns regarding all three.
Request for Regulatory Review
Due to the billing error concerns services not delivered, not agreed to, and the dispute
includes evidence contradicting the merchant’s defenses:
- I respectfully request regulatory review of whether the bank’s investigation satisfied the
reasonable-investigation requirement under 12 CFR §1026.13(f) and whether the bank
relied on merchant assertions of terms presented months after the transaction, without
verifying that those terms were legally enforceable.
Since the dispute concerns goods or services that were not delivered
unambiguously agreed, and the record contains evidence contradicting the
merchant’s assertions:
I respectfully request review of whether the issuer’s investigation met the
reasonable-investigation standard required by 12 CFR §1026.13(f). In

particular, I ask that the Bureau evaluate whether the determination relied
primarily on merchant representations without independently assessing
the consumer’s evidence or verifying the validity of the contractual terms
asserted by the merchant.
Requested Resolution
I respectfully request that First Interstate Bank:
1. Reopen the billing-error investigation.
2. Conduct a compliant investigation under the Fair Credit Billing Act and Regulation Z.
3. Evaluate whether the merchant can substantiate both the delivery of the promised
service and the existence of a legally valid agreement governing the transaction.
4. Reverse / reimburse the cardholder the disputed $10,500 charge and all associated
interest, with confirmation in writing of the details and numbers in total
Sincerely, and respectfully,
Alan Nguyen
Prismatic LLC

Legal Context - Enforceability of Online
Terms
Courts consistently hold that online terms are not enforceable where a consumer is not
provided reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and does not provide a clear and
unambiguous manifestation of assent.
Core Legal Principle
Courts begin with a basic rule of contract law: a contract requires mutual assent
(agreement).
If a user did not receive reasonable notice of the terms and did not clearly and
unambiguously manifest assent to those terms, courts find that no contract was formed at
all.
Plain meaning:
A company cannot enforce hidden terms or policies if the customer was never clearly shown
those terms and never clearly agreed to them.
If the user never agreed to the Terms of Use, none of the provisions within those terms are
binding.
These decisions reinforce the established rule that browsewrap-style terms presented
without clear notice and affirmative acceptance do not create enforceable agreements.
Key Court Decisions
Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc.
763 F.3d 1171 (9th Cir. 2014)
The Ninth Circuit affirmed denial of arbitration, holding that a browsewrap hyperlink alone -
even when visible near checkout buttons - is insufficient to bind a user absent actual
notice or an unambiguous manifestation of assent.

The court ruled that a hyperlink to “Terms of Use” on a website is not enough to bind a
user when the user is not required to click agreement or otherwise clearly indicate
acceptance of the terms.
Plain meaning:
Simply placing terms somewhere online on a website does not automatically make them
binding if the customer never clearly and knowingly agreed to them.
Specht v. Netscape Communications Corp.
150 F. Supp. 2d 585 (S.D.N.Y. 2001)
The court held that downloading software without being required to affirmatively click “I
agree” does not constitute assent to hidden or inconspicuous license terms, and
therefore arbitration could not be compelled.
The court specifically held that downloading software alone is not an unambiguous
manifestation of assent, particularly where the license terms were not clearly presented to the
user.
Plain meaning:
A company cannot claim you agreed to hidden terms simply because you used or downloaded
something.
Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC
More recently, the Ninth Circuit reaffirmed that consumers cannot be bound by contractual
or arbitration provisions embedded in website terms where the interface fails to clearly
inform users that they are agreeing to those terms and fails to obtain an unambiguous
manifestation of assent.
The court emphasized that website terms are enforceable only when users receive clear
notice of the terms and provide unambiguous agreement to them.
Where terms are hidden, buried in hyperlinks, or never clearly accepted by the user, courts
find that no binding agreement exists.

Required Elements for Enforceable
Website Terms
Courts consistently require both of the following:
1. Reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms, and
2. An unambiguous manifestation of assent by the user
If either element is missing, courts generally refuse enforcement.
If a website does not provide reasonably conspicuous notice of the terms and obtain the
user’s clear and unambiguous agreement, courts generally find that no contract was
formed and the terms are not enforceable.
Hidden terms, buried hyperlinks, or terms that a user never clearly and affirmatively agreed to
are therefore not binding.
Courts routinely refuse to enforce website terms where the interface design fails to provide
sufficiently conspicuous notice and fails to obtain clear and unambiguous user assent.
U.S. Supreme Court Principle
These rulings reflect a foundational contract principle recognized by the U.S. Supreme Court:
arbitration is a matter of consent and cannot be imposed absent a valid agreement.
See AT&T Technologies, Inc. v. Communications Workers of America:
“Arbitration is a matter of contract and a party cannot be required to submit to
arbitration any dispute which he has not agreed so to submit.”
Plain meaning:
A company cannot force someone into contract terms they never clearly and knowingly
agreed to.
What This Means for Online Terms

If a court finds that a user never clearly and unambiguously agreed to the Terms of Use, the
entire set of terms may be unenforceable, including, but not limited to:
● deliverables
● arbitration clauses
● liability limitations
● class action waivers
● merchant “terms and conditions” or policy provisions
Plain meaning:
If the customer never clearly agreed to the terms, the company cannot rely on those terms to
limit responsibility or enforce policies.
Bottom Line
Courts repeatedly reject attempts by companies to enforce website terms or policies when:
● the terms were hidden or difficult to notice, and
● the consumer never provided clear and unambiguous agreement to those terms
Simple takeaway:
Hidden terms or “you agreed by using the website” policies often do not hold up in court
without clear notice and unambiguous consent.
Judicial Standard Applied by Courts
Courts evaluating online terms repeatedly emphasize that a user must be placed on clear notice
of the terms and must provide an unambiguous manifestation of assent before those terms
become binding. As the Second Circuit explained in Specht v. Netscape Communications
Corp.:
“A reasonably prudent Internet user would not have known or learned of the
existence of the license terms … and therefore did not unambiguously manifest
assent to those terms.”
This principle is repeatedly applied in cases such as Nguyen v. Barnes & Noble Inc. and
Berman v. Freedom Financial Network, LLC, where courts refused to enforce website terms
because users were not given clear notice and did not clearly agree to them.

Plain meaning:
If a person online does not clearly understand that they are agreeing to specific terms, and are
proven to have agreed to those specific terms, courts generally find that no binding agreement
exists.
If a person online is not unambiguously made aware of clearly specific terms, and clearly,
indisputably consents to & agrees with those specific terms, courts find that no binding
agreement exists.
CONCLUSION / SUMMARY
Courts do not ask whether a merchant technically displayed terms somewhere on a website.
Instead, they examine whether a reasonably prudent user was placed on reasonably
conspicuous notice of specific terms and clearly and unambiguously manifested assent to those
terms.
If a user is not clearly made aware of specific terms from the user’s perspective and experience,
and does not clearly and unambiguously agree to those terms, courts generally find that no
binding agreement exists - regardless of whether the merchant claims the terms were
technically displayed, delivered, or merely available on the website.
Availability of terms alone does not create assent; clear notice and unambiguous agreement are
required for a binding contract to exist.

Dear Mr. Nguyen:
The Division of Banking and Financial Institutions (Division) has received a response from First Interstate Bank (FIB)
regarding your complaint. A copy of the response is attached for your review.
It is the Division’s understanding that this complaint stems from your concerns regarding FIB’s handling of your credit
card billing dispute and your allegation that the bank failed to conduct a reasonable investigation and violated
Regulation Z, 12 CFR 1026.13, and UDAAP standards in connection with a “services not rendered” dispute for a
Done-For-You Airbnb service.
Your first concern as described in your complaint is that FIB failed to conduct a reasonable investigation under
Regulation Z and improperly denied your services-not-rendered billing-error claim despite your position that no Airbnb
property was acquired, no listing went live, no revenue was generated, and the merchant’s partial refund
acknowledged non-delivery. Be advised that, according to the bank’s response and supporting timeline, the bank
opened your dispute, requested additional information from you on August 20, 2025, received your response on
September 5, 2025, obtained the merchant’s representment on October 30, 2025, and issued its decision within
applicable time frames, documenting that it reviewed both your materials and the merchant’s evidence (including
claimed service hours, property analyses, and access to software tools) before concluding that a billing error did not
occur. Regulation Z requires a timely investigation, written explanation, and, upon request, access to documentation; it
does not require the issuer to reach a particular substantive outcome or to resolve contractual disputes over the scope
or quality of services in the consumer’s favor.
Your second concern as described in your complaint is that FIB failed to provide proper notice by relying solely on
physical mail, including a November 14, 2025 “need more information” or rebuttal-response letter you state you never
received, and that the bank’s use of mail and internal deadlines resulted in a constructive denial of your rights under
Regulation Z and constituted unfair or abusive practices. Be advised that the bank’s response indicates that all
dispute-related correspondence was mailed to your address of record and that you had previously received other
dispute letters at that address, and there is no indication that mail was returned as undeliverable or sent outside the
regulatory investigation window. Regulation Z permits issuers to provide required notices by mail to the consumer’s
last known address and does not mandate electronic delivery or require reopening a dispute solely because a
consumer later asserts non-receipt of a particular letter, absent evidence that the issuer failed to send notice in a
timely manner or to the proper address.
After a thorough review of the information provided by you and by FIB, including the dispute timeline, correspondence,

processed the claim, and 90+ days after the case was initially opened Aug 13th 2025. Is this REASONABLE? Or
UNREASONABLE? <7 days while traveling vs 60-90+ days? (I still never physically received the letter) 
ALSO
It is worth mentioning that the original claim was closed in the letter saying that it was “ineligible due to card type” from
the FIB first letters… this needs to be evaluated as well since FIB keeps trying to cheat its way out of evidence based
investigations; focused more on procedural ways to deny a case. 
The fact of the matter is that there are banking laws as well as FTC laws that protect consumers via chargebacks
under UDAAP, REG Z, misrepresentation, fraudulent inducement, non delivery (bait n switch), etc.
- there are also laws out in place for REASONABLE investigation AND REASONABLE time to respond 
- finable penalties stack for willful misconduct, willful negligence, willful non compliance, reckless disregard
- finable penalties can accrue daily 
You said REG Z requires access to documentation upon request, yet I have verbally over recorded calls + in writing
requested this multiple times on multiple different accounts. The bank has refused access to and has not provided any
documentation from the merchant, as requested many times. Even verifiable through the CFPB complaints, and
certified mail + signature with mailed package content proof.
Regarding your statement that “Regulation Z requires a timely investigation, written explanation, and, upon request,
access to documentation; it does not require the issuer to reach a particular substantive outcome or to resolve
contractual disputes over the scope or quality of services in the consumer’s favor” 
It does not exonerate FIB, because FIB has failed the very requirements Reg Z does impose:
1. Access to documentation upon request - directly violated.
Reg Z §1026.13(f) requires the issuer to provide documentation upon the consumer’s request. I have requested
merchant documentation repeatedly - verbally on recorded calls, in writing, and through the CFPB portal. FIB has
refused every time. This is not a gray area. This is a named, specific statutory obligation FIB has willfully not fulfilled.
2. Timely investigation - violated by procedural manipulation.
FIB issued a denial based on a sub-7-day alleged response window while I was traveling - travel FIB had on record
through multiple card-unlock calls, recordings, and their own fraud monitoring system. The merchant received 60- 90+
days. I received fewer than 7, with no verified proof the letter was ever sent or received. That is not a timely and
reasonable process - it is a procedural trap.

This conduct raises serious compliance concerns under the following federal consumer-protection statutes:
Truth in Lending Act (TILA) - 15 U.S.C. §1666
Billing-error investigations must be conducted reasonably and documentary evidence relied upon must be provided
upon request.
Civil liability under 15 U.S.C. §1640 may include:
• Actual damages
• Statutory damages up to $5,000 in individual actions involving credit card billing violations (courts have discretion
depending on the violation)
• Attorney’s fees and litigation costs
Consumer Financial Protection Act (Dodd-Frank Act) - 12 U.S.C. §5531 (UDAAP)
Prohibits unfair, deceptive, or abusive acts or practices by covered financial institutions.
Civil penalties assessed by regulators may reach:
• Up to $5,000 per day for ordinary violations
• Up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations
• Up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations
Additionally, continued enforcement of a disputed charge where the investigation process itself is materially defective
raises concerns regarding reckless disregard of regulatory obligations.
Given the undisputed documentary record demonstrating that requested evidence was withheld during the
investigation and only produced after regulatory escalation, continued enforcement of this charge significantly
increases legal and regulatory exposure.

1) Fair Credit Billing Act (FCBA) - Investigation Requirements
Fair Credit Billing Act - 15 U.S.C. §1666
“If a creditor receives a written notice of a billing error… the creditor shall investigate the matter and report the results
of such investigation to the obligor.” - 15 U.S.C. §1666(a)
2) Regulation Z - Mandatory Investigation Standard
Federal regulation implementing the FCBA.
“The creditor shall conduct a reasonable investigation of the billing error.” - 12 CFR §1026.13(f)  
3) Reg Z - Must Explain Why Dispute Was Denied
If the bank denies the dispute, they must explain why.
Verbatim rule “The creditor shall mail or deliver to the consumer an explanation of why the creditor believes the billing
error did not occur.” - 12 CFR §1026.13(f)(1)
4) FTC Act - Unfair or Deceptive Acts
 “Unfair or deceptive acts or practices in or affecting commerce… are hereby declared unlawful.” - 15 U.S.C. §45(a)
(1) 
5) CFPB / Dodd-Frank - UDAAP (Binding on Banks)
Applies directly to banks.
“The Bureau may take action to prevent a covered person… from committing or engaging in an unfair, deceptive, or
abusive act or practice.”
....
When a Chargeback Investigation Becomes “Unfair” - Based on regulators’ interpretation of the above statutes.
A bank investigation can become unfair or deceptive if it:
- Ignores material evidence
- Relies solely on merchant claims
- Fails to conduct a reasonable investigation

For clarity, the issues raised in this submission relate specifically to the statutory obligations imposed on credit-card
issuers when investigating billing-error disputes.
Under the Fair Credit Billing Act, creditors are required to conduct an investigation and report the results of that
investigation to the consumer.
Fair Credit Billing Act
15 U.S.C. §1666(a)
“If a creditor receives a written notice of a billing error… the creditor shall investigate the matter and report the results
of such investigation to the obligor.”
Source
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/15/1666
Federal regulations implementing this statute further require that the creditor perform a reasonable investigation.
Regulation Z
12 CFR §1026.13(f)
“The creditor shall conduct a reasonable investigation of the billing error.”
Source
https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/

Gmail - Consumer Complaint - First Interstate Bank 3/7/26, 7:40 PM
https://mail.google.com/mail/u/0/?ik=6ad66b5443&view=pt&search=…l=msg-a:r-1360893119810118358&simpl=msg-a:r2979508502533843971 Page 15 of 18
Additionally, if the creditor determines that no billing error occurred, the creditor must provide the consumer with an
explanation of that determination.
“The creditor shall mail or deliver to the consumer an explanation of why the creditor believes the billing error did not
occur.”
 12 CFR §1026.13(f)(1)
Source
https://www.consumerfinance.gov/rules-policy/regulations/1026/13/
The concerns raised in this complaint relate to whether these statutory investigation obligations were satisfied in light
of the documentary record.
Given the above statutory framework, the core issue presented is not whether the bank was required to rule in the
consumer’s favor, but whether the investigation conducted satisfied the legal requirement that a creditor “conduct a
reasonable investigation of the billing error” under Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13(f)) and properly report the results
of that investigation under the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666).
The documentation recently produced after regulatory escalation materially affects the integrity of that investigation
and warrants further supervisory review.
I require immediate full refund of the $10,500 + all associated interest charges, and retraining of FIB to conduct true

consumer chargeback rights, etc.

refused or failed to provide the records it claimed to have relied upon. The
requested materials were only produced after intervention by the Division of

in part on a merchant-submitted “terms and agreement” that was never
provided to or acknowledged by the consumer at the time of purchase. The
prolonged refusal to provide the investigation records raises concerns
regarding the bank’s dispute documentation practices, record retention, and
whether the investigation referenced in prior communications was supported

Regulation Z Compliance Failure - Issuer
Denied Billing Error While Refusing to
Produce Evidence Relied Upon
Attached is the details of what happened, in addition to a large packet of documented
evidence, proofs, and communication history with First Interstate Bank (FIB)
Summary
This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s refusal to produce the investigation
documentation it claimed to have relied upon in denying a billing-error dispute, despite
repeated documented requests over an approximately seven-month period.
The issuer represented that merchant documentation and internal records were used to support
its determination. However, despite requests submitted through certified mail, fax
transmissions, and recorded telephone calls, the bank refused or failed to produce the
materials it claimed to have relied upon.
The requested investigation documentation was only produced after intervention by the
Division of Banking and Financial Institutions (DBFI).
The prolonged refusal to produce the materials relied upon in the dispute determination raises a
separate regulatory concern regarding the issuer’s dispute documentation practices,
record retention, and whether the investigation referenced in prior communications with
regulators was supported by the underlying records at the time the determination was
made.
Core Regulatory Issue
The central issue is issuer compliance with federal billing-error investigation
requirements, not simply the underlying merchant dispute.

Under Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13), issuers must:
● conduct a reasonable investigation
● determine whether a billing error occurred
● explain the determination
● provide documentation relied upon when requested
If the issuer cannot produce the documents it relied upon, the determination cannot be
independently verified as compliant with Regulation Z.
Undisputed Timeline
Aug 13, 2025
Billing dispute opened.
Sept 5, 2025
Complete dispute documentation submitted.
Sept 25, 2025
Provisional credit issued.
Oct 30, 2025
Issuer states merchant provided rebuttal documentation.
Nov 14, 2025
Issuer claims a “Need More Information” letter was mailed.
Nov 24, 2025
Issuer claims dispute closed for non-response.
However:
● I never received the alleged November 14 letter
● the issuer cannot provide proof of mailing or delivery
● the issuer refuses to provide the merchant documentation it relied upon
Regulatory Deficiencies

1. Refusal to Produce Documentation Relied Upon
The issuer states that merchant documentation was used to deny the dispute.
Despite repeated written requests, the issuer has not produced:
● the complete merchant representment packet
● attachments referenced in the denial
● the dispute case log with timestamps
● the internal review documentation supporting the decision
Without production of these materials, the reasonableness of the investigation required under
12 CFR §1026.13 cannot be independently verified.
An investigation that cannot be verified based on documentary evidence raises a material
compliance concern.
Consumer Financial Protection Act (CFPA) Compliance
Concerns
The conduct described above raises potential compliance concerns under the Consumer
Financial Protection Act’s prohibition on unfair, deceptive, or abusive acts or practices
(UDAAP).
● 12 USC 5531 (UDAAP)
● 12 USC 5565 (civil penalties)
Consumer Financial Protection Act
Under the statute:
“The Bureau may take any action… to prevent a covered person from committing or
engaging in an unfair, deceptive, or abusive act or practice.”
Source
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5531
The prolonged refusal to provide investigation documentation relied upon in a dispute
determination, despite repeated documented requests and subsequent regulatory intervention,

raises concerns regarding whether the issuer’s dispute-handling practices could result in
avoidable consumer harm or procedural unfairness.
Potential Regulatory Enforcement Authority
If supervisory review identifies violations of the Consumer Financial Protection Act, the Bureau
has authority to pursue civil penalties under:
Consumer Financial Protection Act
Civil penalty tiers authorized by statute include:
• Up to $5,000 per day for ordinary violations
• Up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations
• Up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing violations
Source
https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/12/5565
These penalties apply when regulators determine that covered financial institutions have
engaged in conduct that violates federal consumer financial law.
Additional Procedural Deficiencies and
Notice Issues
The issuer’s failure to produce the documentation relied upon occurred despite repeated
documented requests over an extended period of time.
Specifically, the requested investigation materials were sought through multiple channels,
including:
• Certified mail correspondence
• Fax transmissions
• Recorded telephone calls with bank representatives

Despite these repeated documented requests, the issuer refused or failed to produce the
requested materials for approximately seven months following the initial dispute filed in
August 2025.
The requested documentation was only produced after intervention by the state regulator,
the Division of Banking and Financial Institutions (DBFI).
Prior to that regulatory involvement, the issuer ignored or declined multiple consumer requests
for the materials it claims to have relied upon in denying the billing error.
This extended refusal to produce investigation documentation further prevents independent
verification that a reasonable investigation occurred as required under Regulation Z (12 CFR
§1026.13).
The fact that the documentation was produced only after regulatory intervention further
raises questions regarding the issuer’s repeated patterns of dispute-handling procedures
and recordkeeping practices.
Notice and Opportunity to Respond
The issuer also closed the dispute for “non-response” based on an alleged November 14, 2025
letter, which the issuer claims was mailed.
However:
• I never received the alleged letter
• the issuer has not produced proof of mailing received or delivery confirmed to be
received by client.
• the issuer provided no alternative form of notice
The issuer also denied any digital documentation to be provided upon, as well as
sending the documentation to an alternative address, upon multiple requests.
This issue is particularly concerning because the issuer had actual knowledge that I was
traveling at the time.
While traveling, the bank placed multiple fraud locks on my credit card, requiring me to
contact the bank repeatedly to restore access to the account.
During these calls:

• I spoke with live bank representatives on recorded lines
• I was required to explain multiple times that I was traveling
• I provided the specific states I was traveling to in order to lift the fraud restrictions.
Accordingly, the issuer had direct knowledge that I was traveling and had recently
communicated with the bank through recorded calls.
Despite that knowledge, the issuer:
• relied solely on a single physical mail notice
• provided fewer than approximately 10 days to respond
• closed the dispute for non-response without proof that the notice was delivered
Closing a billing-error dispute under these circumstances raises serious concerns regarding
whether the consumer was given a meaningful opportunity to respond.
2. Closure of Dispute Without Proof of Notice
The issuer claims a “Need More Information” letter was mailed on November 14, 2025.
However, the issuer has not produced:
● proof of delivery.
● tracking number
● certified mail receipt
● Signature of mail received
Closing a billing-error dispute for “non-response” without proof that the consumer received
notice deprives the consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond.
In addition to this, the consumer initiated the chargeback in August of 2025, and this alleged
letter from the issuer was dated 3+ months later, while the client has communicated multiple
times that they were traveling, while given no notice, and less than 10 days to respond due to
included alleged travel time for mail. Assuming standard 3 day mailing processing and handling
time, the client would have had less than 7 days with no notice to a mailbox (never received)
over 1000+ miles away from the clients confirmed travel locations, confirmed and
communicated over recorded calls multiple times; also verified by fraud lock and lift history on
the credit card. This deprives the consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond.

3. Reliance on Unsupported Merchant Assertions
The issuer relied on merchant claims that consultation or temporary access to tools constituted
fulfillment of the service.
However, the promised service was:
Done-For-You Airbnb property acquisition, furnishing, listing launch, and revenue
generation.
The undisputed facts show:
● no property acquired
● no Airbnb listing launched
● no revenue generated
● merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non-delivery
1 cfpb response final
Preparatory activity or consultation does not constitute delivery of the contracted service.
Core Issue: Disputed Merchant Terms Used to Deny the
Billing Error
During the dispute investigation, the issuer relied on a document the merchant submitted as a
“terms and agreement.” I did not receive, sign, acknowledge, or otherwise consent to that
document at the time of purchase.
The merchant’s own written communications indicate that no agreement was provided at the
time of the transaction. In text messages sent more than one month after the purchase, the
merchant states that an agreement had been omitted when the service was sold.
Despite this, the issuer relied on the merchant-supplied document to conclude that the
transaction was valid and that refunds were restricted. The merchant also attempted to change
the scope of the transaction after payment by:
● reducing the promised service from two properties to one after missed timelines and
non-delivery
● asserting a “no refund” position based on the disputed document
● claiming billable hours that were never agreed to in writing or acknowledged by the
consumer.

These assertions conflict with the merchant’s marketing materials, website representations,
invoice, and written communications describing the service that was originally promised.
Because the issuer relied on a document that was not agreed to by the consumer and that
conflicts with the merchant’s own statements and promotional materials, the determination that
no billing error occurred remains disputed.
Supporting documentation, communications, marketing materials, and the merchant’s written
statements are included in the attached evidence packet.
The issuer has routinely failed to conduct a reasonable investigation as federal law requires.
Systematically and repeatedly denied multiple appeals from the consumer, ignoring all evidence
and denying the claim without performing their obligated duties of such. Including giving the
consumer a meaningful opportunity to respond. All documentation and proof provided.
Supervisory Escalation Concern
The issuer has represented to the CFPB that it relied upon specific documentation in denying
the billing error.
However, despite repeated requests, the issuer has not produced the documentation it
claims to have relied upon, including the merchant representment packet, internal dispute
case log, or proof of mailing of the alleged November 14 notice.
If the issuer cannot produce the documentation underlying its determination, the denial
cannot be independently verified as compliant with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13).
This raises a material question regarding:
● the adequacy of the issuer’s billing-error investigation procedures
● its dispute documentation practices
● whether the representations made to the CFPB are supported by underlying records
For this reason, I respectfully request supervisory review of the issuer’s Regulation Z
dispute-handling and documentation practices in connection with this matter.
Requested Resolution
To correct the billing error and restore the account to the position it would have been in absent
the error, I request:

1. Reversal of the remaining $10,500 charge for services not rendered.
2. Refund of all interest, fees, and finance charges associated with the disputed amount.
3. Written confirmation that the account balance has been corrected.
4. Confirmation that no adverse credit reporting occurred or will occur.
5. Production of the documentation relied upon in the issuer’s determination, including:
○ merchant representment packet
○ internal dispute case log with timestamps
○ proof of mailing or delivery of the alleged November 14 notice.
Closing
The matter remains unresolved because the issuer continues to enforce the disputed charge
while refusing to produce the documentation it claims to have relied upon in denying the
billing error.
If the issuer cannot produce the evidence supporting its determination, the denial cannot be
independently verified as compliant with federal law.
Accordingly, I respectfully request CFPB supervisory review of the issuer’s Regulation Z
compliance in this matter.

YOUR COMPLAINT
On August 13, 2025, I filed a credit card billing dispute with First
Interstate Bank for $10,500 related to a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb
service that was never delivered. I paid $18,000 for a fixed-fee service
promising property acquisition, furnishing, Airbnb listing launch, and
revenue generation. After 11+ weeks, no property, no listing, and no
revenue existed. The merchant acknowledged non-delivery by issuing a
$7,500 partial refund, leaving $10,500 outstanding. I submitted complete
dispute documentation on September 5, 2025. Despite this, the bank:
asserted “missing documentation” after receipt, relied on the merchant’s
rebuttal instead of verifying delivery, and denied the dispute and appeal
without addressing non-delivery. The bank later closed the dispute for
“non-response” based on a physical letter I never received, despite
knowing I was traveling and despite my repeated requests for digital
delivery. No alternative notice was provided. The bank also relied on
unsigned, post-payment merchant terms that I never agreed to. There is no
evidence of assent. Such terms cannot cure non-delivery. These actions

conduct a reasonable investigation and under Dodd-Frank UDAAP for
unfair procedural handling causing consumer harm. I am requesting
CFPB review of the bank’s compliance and assistance obtaining: refund
of the remaining $10,500, and reversal of associated interest and fees.

On August 13, 2025, I filed a credit card billing dispute with First Interstate Bank for
$10,500 related to a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service that was never delivered. I paid
$18,000 for a fixed-fee service promising property acquisition, furnishing, Airbnb listing
launch, and revenue generation. After 11+ weeks, no property, no listing, and no revenue
existed. The merchant acknowledged non-delivery by issuing a $7,500 partial refund, leaving
$10,500 outstanding. I submitted complete dispute documentation on September 5, 2025.
Despite this, the bank: asserted “missing documentation” after receipt, relied on the
merchant’s rebuttal instead of verifying delivery, and denied the dispute and appeal without
addressing non-delivery. The bank later closed the dispute for “non-response” based on a

unsigned, post-payment merchant terms that I never agreed to. There is no evidence of
assent. Such terms cannot cure non-delivery. These actions raise concerns under Regulation

UDAAP for unfair procedural handling causing consumer harm. I am requesting CFPB
review of the bank’s compliance and assistance obtaining: refund of the remaining $10,500,
and reversal of associated interest and fees. Further details, documents, and information

What would be a fair resolution to this issue?
A fair resolution is correction of a documented services-not-rendered billing error and
restoration of my account to the position it would have been in had the error not occurred.
Specifically, I am requesting: Immediate refund of the remaining $10,500 for the undelivered
service Reversal of all interest and fees associated with the disputed amount Written
confirmation that the dispute is resolved on the merits (non-delivery), not consumer fault
Confirmation that no adverse or retaliatory action will be taken against my account for
exercising dispute rights This resolution reflects the minimum corrective action required
under Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and avoids unnecessary escalation. I reserve all
rights related to consequential damages arising from this matter.

ADDITIONAL COMMENTS
Supplemental Statement - Timeline Clarification & Regulatory Compliance
(Hybrid Addendum) First Interstate Bank’s CFPB response relies on
generalized references to “timeframes” while refusing to disclose the actual
dates necessary to verify compliance with federal law and card-network
standards. The undisputed timeline is as follows: Aug 13, 2025 - Billing
dispute opened Sept 5, 2025 - Complete dispute documentation submitted
Sept 25, 2025 - Provisional credit issued Nov 13, 2025 - Merchant rebuttal
dated Nov 14, 2025 - Bank claims notice mailed (never received) Nov 24,
2025 - Alleged response deadline This reflects approximately 92 days from
dispute initiation to the merchant rebuttal date. Despite this, the consumer
was allegedly given ~9 days to respond, via physical mail only, during a
period when the bank had confirmed knowledge that the consumer was
traveling and had repeatedly requested digital delivery. First Interstate
Bank has not disclosed: the date the merchant rebuttal was received, the
date it was processed, or the date it was reviewed. Without these dates,
neither the consumer nor the CFPB can verify compliance with: Regulation
Z (12 CFR §1026.13) - reasonable investigation and meaningful notice
requirements, Mastercard dispute processing standards for Goods or
Services Not Provided, or Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards, which prohibit
unfair procedural structures that cause avoidable consumer harm. Further,
the bank continues to rely on a “consultation = fulfillment” theory and
unsigned, post-payment merchant terms while ignoring undisputed facts:
no property was acquired, no Airbnb listing went live, no revenue was
generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging nondelivery. Closing a dispute under these circumstances reflects procedural
denial rather than an evidence-based determination. I am requesting:
Disclosure of the exact receipt, processing, and review dates for the
merchant rebuttal; and Reconsideration of the dispute based on
documented non-delivery, not unsigned merchant assertions. Absent
correction, this matter presents ongoing Reg Z and UDAAP compliance
concerns appropriate for supervisory review.

YOUR COMPLAINT
Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing Violations This complaint concerns
First Interstate Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence,
reckless disregard, and willful noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP
standards in handling my credit card billing dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-
28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction. This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural
and substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate Bank denied a services-notrendered dispute without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No property was
delivered, no listing launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging nondelivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank
relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and
refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of
compliance with Regulation Z. The bank gave the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025
to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days, by physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and
after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was
closed anyway. The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines have passed. This is legally
false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure when the investigation
is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies
merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or
consultation does not constitute delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof
provided on the subject in detail, directly from the merchant themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853,
the test is binary: Was the promised service delivered? Here, the answer is no. The promised service with provided
proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb acquired property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits
$3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No
timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank continues to demonstrate willful negligence, and
routinely ignores action relating to directly addressed compliance with UDAAP, REG Z, proper investigation, etc.
They refuse to answer compliance related questions, and continue to violate consumer chargeback rights, and are
abusing their powers as a bank, due to a lack of due diligence, and repeated documented willful misconduct. I am
requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP
violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB oversight. This must be escalated to all relevant
enforcement agencies.

Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence, reckless disregard, and willful
noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in handling my credit card billing
dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction.
This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural and substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate
Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No
property was delivered, no listing launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging nondelivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank relied on
unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the
dates the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z. The bank gave
the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days, by
physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged
November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines
have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure when the
investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies
merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does
not constitute delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail,
directly from the merchant themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service

activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank continues to demonstrate

What would be a fair resolution to this issue?

matter. 6. No retaliation 7. Compliance with consumer rights, UDAAP, Reg Z, & chargeback rights. 8. Retraining of First Interstate Bank

ADDITIONAL COMMENTS
CFPB Case 260217-28952925 - Consumer Feedback The issuer upheld denial of a billing error dispute while
refusing to produce the documentation it relied upon, closing the dispute without proof of proper notice, and
making a factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records that may require verification. Despite
repeated direct requests, no executed agreement, no merchant representment packet, and no proof of mailing of the
November 14 letter has been produced. These deficiencies prevent independent verification of investigation
adequacy under 12 CFR §1026.13. The materials identified below have been requested multiple times directly from
the bank. The bank has refused or failed to produce them. 1. Executed Governing Agreement Not Produced The
denial relies on alleged merchant terms including hourly billing, non-refundability, and modified service
characterization. Despite repeated written requests, the bank has not produced: - Any signed governing agreement -
Any signed authorization for hourly billing - Any executed agreement establishing non-refundable terms - Any
document evidencing assent to modified deliverables If the issuer cannot produce an executed agreement supporting
the terms relied upon, the investigation cannot be independently verified as reasonable under 12 CFR §1026.13. An
investigation that relies on unsigned or unexecuted merchant assertions is not independently supportable without
documentary proof. 2. Merchant Representment Packet Withheld After Repeated Requests The bank states the
merchant responded on October 30, 2025 and that it relied upon those materials in denying the dispute. I have
repeatedly requested: - The complete merchant representment packet - All attachments referenced - The full internal
dispute case log with date/time stamps These materials have not been produced. Regulation Z requires that, upon
request, the issuer provide copies of documents relied upon in making its determination. Continued refusal or
failure to provide the documentation relied upon prevents meaningful review of the investigation. If the issuer
cannot produce the documentation it relied upon, the denial lacks a verifiable evidentiary basis. 3. November 14,
2025 Letter - No Proof of Mailing or Delivery The bank asserts a “Need More Information” letter was mailed
November 14, 2025 and that the dispute was closed due to non-response. I have repeatedly requested documented
proof of mailing and delivery. None has been provided. The bank has not produced: - Proof of mailing method -
Tracking number - Certified mail receipt - Proof of delivery or returned mail - Copy of envelope and address used
Closing a dispute for non-response without proof that notice was properly mailed and delivered deprives the
consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond. If proof of mailing and delivery cannot be produced, the
procedural basis for closing the dispute is not independently supportable. 4. Statement Regarding Travel Notices -
Verification Required The bank asserts it has “no record of a travel notice.” I called multiple times on recorded lines
to clear fraud flags while traveling and notified the bank of my travel states on multiple occasions. Because the
issuer has made a factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records, preservation and review of: - All
call recordings - Fraud department notes - Internal account annotations - Travel-notice logs is necessary to verify
the accuracy of statements made in this response. If internal recordings or logs reflect travel notifications, the
statement made to the CFPB would be materially inaccurate. 5. Investigation Adequacy Under 12 CFR §1026.13
The denial appears based on merchant narrative rather than documented proof of agreed contractual terms or actual
delivery of the promised service. Without production of: - The executed governing agreement - The documentation
relied upon in denial - The complete representment file - The dispute case log with timestamps Proof of proper
notice the reasonableness of the investigation cannot be independently evaluated. Once these deficiencies were
raised and documentation was repeatedly requested, continued refusal to produce the materials relied upon raises a
material question regarding compliance with 12 CFR §1026.13. Requested CFPB Action I respectfully request that
the CFPB require the issuer to produce: - Signed governing agreement relied upon - Signed hourly billing
authorization (if claimed) - Complete merchant representment packet with attachments - Complete dispute case log
