FINAL SUPPLEMENTAL COMPLIANCE
DEMAND
Re: CFPB Case 260131-28391819 | Claim D2522303892
Regulation Z • Dodd-Frank UDAAP • Mastercard Reason Code 4853
First Interstate Bank’s February 11, 2026 response fails to cure - and instead compounds -
material violations of federal consumer protection law.
This submission is intended to force administrative correction, not debate.
1. Timeline Compliance Does NOT Cure Substantive
Illegality
Even assuming, arguendo, that the merchant’s representment was received within internal or
network timelines (not conceded), timeline compliance is legally irrelevant where:
• no goods or services were delivered
• the investigation ignored undisputed non-delivery
• denial relied on unsigned, post-payment terms
• and outcome logic ratified merchant non-performance
There is no safe harbor under:
● Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13),
● Mastercard dispute standards, or
● Dodd-Frank UDAAP
that permits denying a services-not-rendered claim based on procedure alone.
A procedurally timely denial that ignores non-delivery is still unlawful.
2. Partial Refund + “Scaling” Is a Legal Admission of
Non-Delivery
Undisputed facts:
• Merchant sold a two-property Done-For-You Airbnb package
• Merchant failed to deliver any property
• Merchant unilaterally “scaled” the package from two properties to one upon missed
promised timelines and lack of delivery of promised DFY profiting Airbnb
properties.
• Consumer did not agree to, sign, or consent to this modification, or ANY

documentation.
• Merchant issued a $7,500 refund on July 7, 2025
• Merchant retained $10,500 without contractual authority
This matters because:
A merchant cannot both:
1. refund part of a transaction for non-delivery, and
2. claim the remainder is “non-refundable” under unsigned terms
The refund is an admission of failure of consideration.
Once consideration fails:
• refund limitations collapse
• post-payment terms are void
• chargeback rights remain intact
First Interstate Bank enforced a non-contract.
3. “Consultation + Software = Fulfillment” Is Legally
Defective
The essential purpose of the transaction was clear and documented:
Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition, launch, and income generation ($3,500-$5,000+ profit
per property after all operational expenses)
Undisputed delivery record:
• no lease
• no property
• no listing
• no revenue
Temporary access to:
• AirDNA, or
• marketplaces, or
• consultation
does not constitute delivery of the contracted DFY service.
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary:
Was the promised service delivered?
Here, the answer is no.
Token or preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery.

4. Enforcement of Unsigned, Post-Payment Terms Is
Unlawful
First Interstate Bank relied on merchant-submitted terms that were:
• unsigned
• introduced after payment
• never disclosed at checkout
• never accepted by the consumer
There is:
• no signed agreement
• no clickwrap record
• no assent evidence
Unsigned, post-payment terms are unenforceable as a matter of basic contract law and
cannot defeat statutory billing error rights.
By enforcing them, the bank substituted merchant narrative for lawful agreement.
5. Procedural Closure Without Meaningful Notice Violates
Reg Z
The dispute was closed for “non-response” even though:
• notice was sent physical mail only
• the bank knew the consumer was traveling
• repeated digital delivery requests were refused
• receipt was never confirmed
A dispute cannot be closed for non-response where notice was not meaningfully delivered.
This is constructive denial of notice, not consumer default.
6. Outcome Logic Is the Core Violation
This case was denied not because services were delivered, but because:
• the merchant said “non-refundable”
• the bank enforced unsigned terms
• the bank avoided the delivery question
That logic violates:
• Regulation Z’s reasonable investigation requirement
• UDAAP’s prohibition on unfair procedural structures
• Mastercard’s prohibition on curing non-delivery with token activity

A denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not compliance.
It is facilitation.
6.A Knowledge, Notice, and Escalation to Willful
Non-Compliance
At this stage, First Interstate Bank’s conduct can no longer be characterized as inadvertent error
or procedural oversight.
The bank has now been placed on actual notice of:
• total non-delivery of the contracted service
• the merchant’s admission of failure via partial refund
• the absence of any signed or accepted refund-limiting terms
• the legal irrelevance of “consultation” to the essential purpose of the transaction
• the constructive denial of notice used to close the dispute
Despite this notice, the bank has affirmatively chosen to:
• continue enforcing unsigned, post-payment merchant assertions
• refuse administrative correction
• continue charging interest on a disputed, non-delivered transaction
Continued refusal to correct the billing error after notice constitutes:
• willful non-compliance with Regulation Z
• reckless disregard for consumer protection obligations
• unfair conduct under Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards
Once notice is provided, continued enforcement of an unlawful outcome is no longer a mistake -
it is a knowing decision.
7. Required Corrective Action
To resolve this matter without further escalation, First Interstate Bank must:
1. Reverse the $10,500 charge
2. Remove all associated interest and fees
3. Confirm the account is restored to pre-dispute status
This is the lowest-risk outcome.
Continued refusal after notice converts this matter from error to knowing non-compliance,
escalating exposure under Regulation Z and UDAAP supervisory standards.

Continued refusal to correct this billing error after formal notice escalates this matter from
dispute resolution into supervisory and enforcement territory, where intent and pattern - not
merchant narratives - control exposure.
8. Final Notice
I am seeking resolution - not conflict.
However, absent immediate correction, I will continue pursuing:
• CFPB supervisory review
• regulatory escalation
• and legal remedies
No timeline argument cures non-delivery.
No unsigned term defeats federal law.
No consultation substitutes for performance.
Any further denial after this submission will constitute knowing ratification of
non-delivery, not procedural compliance.
Refund + interest removal resolves this immediately.

First Interstate Bank Rebuttal Checklist
Claim Number: D2522303892
CFPB Case: 260131-28391819
A. “Policies and procedures were properly followed”
Bank assertion:
“Records indicate that all applicable policies and procedures were properly
followed.”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Policies cited? No. Bank does not identify which policies.
● ☐ Procedures identified? No.
● ☐ Evidence of reasonable investigation under Reg Z? No.
● ☐ Evidence of service delivery? No.
● ☐ Explanation of how outcome was reached based on facts? No.
Regulatory issue:
Compliance cannot be asserted conclusively without disclosing:
● what standards were applied, and
● how evidence was weighed.
A conclusory statement ≠ compliance under 12 CFR §1026.13.
B. “Merchant responded within allowable timeframe”
Bank assertion:
“The merchant provided representment documents, within the allowable
timeframe…”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Date merchant rebuttal received by bank disclosed? No.
● ☐ Date merchant rebuttal processed disclosed? No.
● ☐ Date merchant rebuttal reviewed disclosed? No.
● ☐ Timeline compliance independently verifiable? No.
Key point:
Even if timelines were met, timeline compliance does not excuse:
● denial without evidence of delivery, or
● reliance on unenforceable terms.
C. “Transaction was non-refundable”
Bank assertion:
“…indicating the transaction was non-refundable.”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Signed agreement proving non-refundability? No.
● ☐ Proof consumer assented to refund restriction pre-payment? No.

● ☐ Evidence refund policy overrides non-delivery? No.
● ☐ Consideration of merchant’s own written “pay $0 if no property”? No.
Critical legal flaw:
Refundability is irrelevant where no services were rendered.
A merchant cannot keep payment for a service that never occurred.
D. “Consumer did not respond by November 24”
Bank assertion:
“You did not provide a response by the date requested…”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Proof consumer received November 14 letter? No.
● ☐ Delivery confirmation (USPS / tracking)? No.
● ☐ Alternate delivery attempted (email / portal)? No.
● ☐ Bank acknowledged consumer travel status? Yes.
● ☐ Bank refused digital delivery despite request? Yes.
Regulatory issue:
Closing a dispute for “non-response” without proof of notice violates:
● Reg Z reasonable investigation standard
● UDAAP unfairness (avoidable consumer harm)
E. “Investigation concluded after reviewing documents”
Bank assertion:
“After reviewing the representment documents and all other available supporting
documentation…”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Identifies which documents were relied upon? No.
● ☐ Provides copies of merchant evidence automatically? No.
● ☐ Identifies evidence of service delivery? No.
● ☐ Distinguishes consultation from DFY deployment? No.
Key defect:
The bank reviewed merchant assertions, not performance evidence.
F. “Consultation = fulfillment”
Merchant claim adopted by bank:
“…providing consultation… AirDNA Pro… airbitrage AI…”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Contracted service = consultation? No.
● ☐ Property acquired? No.
● ☐ Lease executed? No.
● ☐ Listing launched? No.
● ☐ Revenue generated? No.

● ☐ Partial refund issued acknowledging failure? Yes.
Conclusion:
Token software access does not satisfy a DFY Airbnb deployment contract.
This is a services-not-rendered dispute under Mastercard standards and Reg Z.
G. “Missing documentation”
Bank assertion:
“…we have not received it…”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Bank acknowledges full packet received Sept 5, 2025? Yes (prior records).
● ☐ Explains what specific document was missing? No.
● ☐ Explains why already-submitted evidence was disregarded? No.
Regulatory issue:
Repeatedly claiming “missing documentation” without specificity is procedurally abusive.
H. “Right to request documents”
Bank assertion:
“You have the right to request a copy of any documents relied upon…”
Checklist Response:
● ☐ Documents provided proactively? No.
● ☐ Merchant rebuttal provided with denial? No.
● ☐ CFPB response included merchant evidence? No.
Key issue:
A consumer should not have to request documents after a decision to understand the basis of
denial.
Core Takeaway (for CFPB / Supervisory Review)
● The bank never identified evidence of service delivery.
● The bank relied on unsigned merchant terms.
● The bank closed the dispute without proof of notice.
● The bank equated consultation with fulfillment.
● The bank asserted compliance without disclosing facts.
This is not a dispute over timing.
This is a substantive failure of investigation and outcome logic.

This submission supplements, and does not replace my prior factual checklist;
and directly responds to First Interstate Bank’s February 11, 2026 CFPB
response. It addresses specific misstatements, omissions, and legal defects that
remain unresolved.
02/16/2026
Point-by-Point Compliance Rebuttal
Checklist
Re: CFPB Case 260131-28391819 | Claim D2522303892
(All dates and statements below are taken directly from First Interstate Bank’s (FIB’s) own
letters via their CFPB response PDF attachment)
1. Claim: “All applicable policies and procedures were
properly followed.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Regulation Z requires a reasonable investigation into whether services were
actually delivered (12 CFR §1026.13).
☐ No FIB letter identifies any evidence that a property was delivered, leased, listed, or \
revenue-producing - as referenced in documented and provided material directly
from merchant.
☐ First Interstate Bank substituted merchant narrative for proof of delivery.
☐ FIB never answered the core question: Were the promised services delivered?
Result: Policy compliance is irrelevant if the outcome violates federal law. Procedure does
not override substance.
2. Claim: “Merchant provided representment documents
within the allowable timeframe.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Dispute opened: 08/13/2025
☐ Merchant rebuttal dated: 11/13/2025
☐ Time elapsed: ~92 days
☐ First Interstate Bank has refused to disclose in writing:
● date rebuttal was received
● date rebuttal was processed

● date rebuttal was reviewed
☐ Consumer response window: ~9 days (11/14-11/24)
☐ Merchant response window: ~92 days
Result: Even if timelines were met (not conceded), timeline compliance is not a safe harbor
under Regulation Z when the investigation ignores undisputed non-delivery.
3. Claim: “Transaction was non-refundable per merchant
terms.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ No signed contract provided
☐ No clickwrap or electronic acceptance record
☐ No proof refund restrictions were disclosed prior to payment
☐ Terms were introduced post-payment and post-non-delivery
☐ Bank relied on merchant-submitted, unsigned terms
☐ Bank treated those terms as binding despite no assent
☐ Terms were introduced after non-delivery, and outside of the awareness or consent of
Consumer
☐ Merchant issued a $7,500 refund, contradicting “non-refundable”
☐ Refund was issued after missed timelines and failure to secure any property
☐ Refund contradicts any claim that the transaction was “non-refundable”
☐ No document exists showing the consumer agreed that the remaining $10,500 would
become non-refundable
☐ No evidence of consumer consent to waive refund rights after partial performance
Failure
The merchant’s issuance of a $7,500 refund constitutes an admission that
the contracted DFY service was not delivered. A transaction cannot be both
“non-refundable” and partially refunded for non-performance.
Once non-delivery is admitted, the merchant cannot unilaterally declare the
remaining balance “non-refundable” absent a signed settlement agreement
or mutual assent. No such agreement exists.
I never signed, agreed to, or consented to any clause stating that a partial
refund would be final or that no further refunds would be issued. The bank
has produced no signed contract, clickwrap record, or contemporaneous
disclosure supporting this claim.
Scaling the service from two properties to one does not cure non-delivery.
Zero properties were delivered. No lease was executed. No listing went live.

No revenue was generated.
A partial refund after failure of consideration confirms non-performance
and strengthens - rather than defeats a services-not-rendered claim.
Retaining the remaining $10,500 after admitting failure constitutes unjust
enrichment and a continuing billing error under Regulation Z.
Legal Reality:
• A partial refund constitutes admission of non-delivery
• A partial refund constitutes modification of any alleged refund policy
• A merchant cannot selectively enforce refund restrictions after conceding failure
• Refundability is legally irrelevant where no services were rendered
• Unsigned, post-payment terms are unenforceable and cannot defeat:
- Services-Not-Rendered claims
- Fraudulent inducement
- Regulation Z billing error rights
- Chargeback rights
Result: First Interstate Bank enforced a non-contract, ignored a merchant admission, and
allowed retention of funds despite total failure of consideration.
4. Claim: “Consumer failed to respond by November 24,
2025.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Notice sent physical mail only
☐ Bank had actual knowledge consumer was traveling
☐ Consumer repeatedly requested digital delivery
☐ Consumer never received the November letter
☐ FIB never confirmed receipt before closing dispute
Legal Standard: A dispute cannot be closed for non-response when notice was not
meaningfully delivered.
Result: This is constructive denial of notice, not consumer default.
5. Claim: “Consultation + software access = fulfillment.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Promised service: Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition & deployment
☐ Delivered service:
● no lease
● no property
● no listing

● no revenue
☐ Third-party software access:
● low cost
● temporary
● unrelated to essential purpose
Mastercard Standard:
Reason Code 4853 is binary: Was the promised service delivered?
- Here, the answer is no.
Result: Token activity cannot cure total non-delivery.
6. Claim: “Claim does not qualify as billing error due to
card type.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Regulation Z applies to credit card billing errors
☐ “Services not rendered” is a recognized billing error
☐ FIB nonetheless issued provisional credit, confirming eligibility
Result: FIB’s own actions contradict this claim.
7. Claim: “Consumer did not provide requested
documentation.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Full documentation submitted: 09/05/2025
☐ Provisional credit issued: 09/25/2025 (acknowledging sufficiency)
☐ Later denials cite “missing information” without identifying what was missing
☐ FIB never reconciles this contradiction
Result: This is a pretextual denial, not an investigation.
8. Claim: “Consumer should work directly with the
merchant.”
Rebuttal Checklist:
☐ Merchant already admitted failure via partial refund
☐ Merchant is asserting unenforceable terms
☐ Chargeback rights exist precisely because merchants refuse refunds

Result: This statement improperly shifts statutory duties back to the consumer.
9. Core Failure Summary (Undisputed Facts)
☐ Payment made
☐ Promised DFY service defined
☐ No delivery occurred
☐ Partial refund issued
☐ Merchant refund issued due to non-delivery, confirming failure of consideration
☐ Remaining $10,500 retained
☐ Dispute closed on procedure, not evidence
This satisfies:
● Services Not Rendered
● Failure of Consideration
● Fraudulent Inducement indicators
10. Regulatory Consequences
Even assuming arguendo that timelines were met:
● Regulation Z still requires a reasonable investigation
● UDAAP prohibits unfair procedural structures
● Mastercard rules prohibit curing non-delivery with token activity
A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not compliance.
Bottom Line
First Interstate Bank’s response:
● avoids the delivery question
● enforces unsigned terms
● withholds required dates
● relies on non-receipt mail
● substitutes narrative for proof
This is exactly the conduct Regulation Z and UDAAP exist to prevent.
compress this into a 2–3 sentence CFPB “killer paragraph”, or
convert it into Yes/No admissions that corner the bank legally.

This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s violations of Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13)
and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in its handling of my credit card billing dispute.
This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819.
That prior complaint addressed the underlying merchant transaction.
This complaint addresses First Interstate Bank’s unlawful dispute handling, procedural
misconduct, and continued noncompliance after notice.
First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence
that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no Airbnb listing
launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging
non-delivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to
charge interest.
The bank relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the
merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received,
processed, or reviewed. Without these records, neither the consumer nor the CFPB can verify
compliance with Regulation Z’s reasonable investigation requirements.
The bank allowed the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov
13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days to respond, exclusively by physical mail, during
a period when the bank knew I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery multiple times.
The bank alleges a letter dated Nov 14, 2025 and closed the dispute for non-response by Nov
24, 2025. I have never received the alleged letters, yet the dispute was closed anyway.
Timeline compliance does not excuse this conduct. Regulation Z requires a reasonable
investigation and an evidence-based determination. There is no safe harbor allowing a
bank to deny a billing error dispute based on procedure when the underlying facts establish
non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not
compliance - it is facilitation.
The core defect is outcome logic. First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute
without any evidence of service delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect.
I am requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These
Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB
oversight.

No Safe Harbor for Timeline Excuses (Regulation Z & UDAAP)
First Interstate Bank continues to assert that it “cannot assist” because the matter is allegedly
outside internal dispute or chargeback timeframes. This position is legally incorrect.
There is no statute, regulation, or card-network rule that permits a bank to deny or refuse
correction of a billing error solely because internal timelines have elapsed, where the denial
itself resulted from defective notice, unreasonable investigation, or reliance on unenforceable
merchant assertions.
Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) does not create a safe harbor for procedural closure. It
requires a reasonable investigation and a determination based on whether the goods or
services were actually delivered. A dispute denied through defective notice or ignored
evidence does not become lawful merely by passage of time.
Any delay cited by the bank is self-created, caused by:
● reliance on physical mail only despite knowledge of my travel status,
● refusal to provide digital delivery,
● refusal to confirm receipt of notices,
● withholding merchant rebuttal documents, and
● continued denial after notice and appeal.
A bank cannot manufacture finality through its own procedural failures, then invoke that
finality to avoid correcting an unlawful outcome.
Further, continued reliance on “expired windows” after notice and appeal constitutes ongoing
misconduct, not a closed issue. Each billing cycle that interest continues to accrue on a
disputed amount represents a new and continuing violation under Regulation Z and an unfair
practice under Dodd-Frank UDAAP.
If First Interstate Bank truly lacked authority to correct this dispute, it would not continue to:
● assess interest,
● communicate determinations,
● respond to CFPB complaints, or
● assert substantive defenses.
The bank plainly retains authority. It is choosing not to act.
Timeline arguments cannot cure:
● denial of a services-not-rendered dispute without evidence of delivery,
● enforcement of unsigned, post-payment terms,
● denial after partial refund admission, or
● procedural denial without meaningful notice.
Accordingly, the “outside the window” rationale is not a defense. It is evidence of willful
noncompliance, reckless disregard, and continuing violations after notice.

Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing
Violations
This complaint concerns First Interstate Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation,
and its willful misconduct, willful negligence, reckless disregard, and willful
noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards in
handling my credit card billing dispute.
This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-28391819. That complaint
addressed the underlying transaction. This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural and
substantive violations during and after the dispute process.
First Interstate Bank denied a services-not-rendered dispute without identifying any evidence
that the contracted service was delivered. No property was delivered, no listing launched, no
revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging non-delivery.
Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest.
The bank relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the
merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received,
processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of compliance with Regulation Z.
The bank gave the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025 to Nov 13,
2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days, by physical mail only, despite knowing I was
traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged November 14,
2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway.
The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines have passed. This is legally
false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure
when the investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A
procedurally timely denial that ratifies merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is
facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does not constitute
delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the
subject in detail, directly from the merchant themselves.
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service
delivered? Here, the answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a
DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb acquired property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits
$3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses.
Token or preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery.
No timeline argument can cure that defect.

I am requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These
Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB
oversight.

Supplemental Dispute Statement - Substantive
Non-Delivery, Regulatory Non-Compliance, and Pending
Litigation
Case ID: D-252-230-3892
This statement supplements my prior submissions and First Interstate Bank’s CFPB response.
Undisputed Timeline (for record clarity)
● Aug 13, 2025: Billing dispute opened
● Sept 5, 2025: Complete dispute documentation submitted
● Sept 25, 2025: Provisional credit issued
● Nov 13, 2025: Merchant rebuttal dated
● Nov 14, 2025: Bank claims notice mailed (never received)
● Nov 24, 2025: Alleged response deadline
This reflects approximately 92 days from dispute initiation to the merchant rebuttal date,
followed by an alleged ~9-day response window via physical mail only, despite my repeated
requests for digital delivery and the bank’s knowledge that I was traveling.
To date, First Interstate Bank has not disclosed:
● the date the merchant rebuttal was received,
● the date it was processed, or
● the date it was reviewed.
Absent these dates, neither I nor regulators can verify compliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR
§1026.13) or applicable card-network standards.

Substantive Non-Delivery (Independent of Timing)
Even assuming arguendo that the bank contends technical compliance with internal timelines,
reversal remains legally required due to total non-delivery of the essential promised
service.
The following facts are undisputed:
● No property or lease was ever obtained
● No Airbnb listing ever went live
● No revenue was ever generated
● The merchant issued a partial refund, acknowledging non-delivery
This transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb service, not consultation, preparation, or
advisory access. Ancillary consultation or software access does not cure total
non-performance.
Under Regulation Z, a billing error exists where services are not delivered as agreed,
regardless of partial or token activity.
Improper Reliance on Unenforceable Merchant Assertions
The bank’s determination relies on:
● Unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, and
● Merchant narrative, rather than objective evidence of delivery.
No signed agreement, clickwrap acceptance, or proof of assent exists. Reliance on
unenforceable, post-payment terms to deny a billing-error claim is inconsistent with basic
contract law and consumer-protection standards.
Regulatory & Litigation Exposure
Closing this dispute under these circumstances raises ongoing concerns under:

● Regulation Z (failure to conduct a reasonable investigation and provide meaningful
notice),
● Dodd-Frank UDAAP (unfair procedural structures causing consumer harm), and
● Card-network rules governing Goods or Services Not Provided.
Separately, civil litigation is now pending and/or imminent, including claims arising from:
● non-delivery of services,
● fraudulent inducement,
● unjust enrichment, and
● improper reliance on unenforceable contract terms.
Should litigation proceed, First Interstate Bank’s dispute handling, evidentiary review, notice
practices, and refusal to disclose material dates will be subject to formal discovery, sworn
testimony, and judicial scrutiny. This includes potential exposure for failure to correct a
billing error after notice, and for procedural conduct that materially impaired my ability to
respond.
I note this not as a threat, but as a factual consequence of continued non-correction.
Request
I am requesting:
1. Administrative correction and reversal of the remaining $10,500, plus associated
interest; and
2. Written disclosure of the merchant rebuttal receipt, processing, and review dates for
completeness of the record.
I continue to seek resolution amicably and without further escalation, and I am providing First
Interstate Bank the opportunity to correct this matter prior to further regulatory or judicial
involvement.

Plain Statement of the Issue 
This matter is straightforward. 
I paid a merchant for a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment service that was 
clearly promised, clearly defined, and never delivered. 
The merchant accepted payment, failed to deliver the promised services, missed all agreed 
timelines, and later attempted to justify non-delivery by asserting “terms” and billing concepts 
that were never presented, never agreed to, and never signed by me. 
Those post-payment terms are unenforceable as a matter of law and cannot be used to 
defeat a services-not-rendered claim. 
Despite this, First Interstate Bank has treated the merchant’s unilateral, unsigned assertions as 
if they were governing contractual terms - even though no valid agreement exists reflecting 
those provisions. 
I have attached extensive, undeniable documentation originating from the merchant itself - 
including advertising, invoices, sales representations, written communications, and recorded 
statements - all of which establish: 
● what was promised 
● that payment was made 
● that delivery never occurred 
This is not a quality dispute or a misunderstanding. 
This is a documented services-not-rendered dispute involving misrepresentation and fraudulent 
inducement. 
Under consumer protection law and Mastercard dispute standards, a merchant cannot cure 
non-delivery by inventing or retroactively enforcing terms that were never agreed to, and a bank 
cannot rely on such terms to deny a valid dispute. 
I am requesting that First Interstate Bank correct this matter immediately by refunding the 
remaining $10,500, plus all associated interest and fees, consistent with federal consumer credit 
law and network dispute rules.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
This submission concerns a billing dispute arising from a paid transaction for a Done-For-You 
Airbnb acquisition and deployment program that was never delivered. 
This dispute is not ambiguous. It is not a quality complaint. It is not a misunderstanding. It is a 
documented services-not-rendered event supported by evidence of: 
● misrepresentation 
● failure of consideration 
● total non-performance 
The merchant accepted payment for a fixed-fee program promising: 
● property acquisition 
● furnishing and deployment 
● professional listing launch 
● immediate profitability 
After more than eleven weeks: 
● no property was acquired 
● no deployment occurred 
● no revenue-producing system existed 
The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery through unsigned post-payment terms and 
token activity unrelated to the essential purpose of the contract. That conduct cannot legally 
cure non-performance. 
The evidentiary record is binary: 
● promised service exists 
● payment exists 
● delivery does not exist 
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), this satisfies 
chargeback eligibility. 
Despite this, the issuing bank denied the dispute and subsequent appeals under a false premise 
that documentation was missing, even though the complete documentation packet had already 
been submitted and confirmed. 
5 
13300438.1.4-22

The dispute was ultimately closed based on an alleged non-response tied to a physical letter the 
consumer never received, during a period when: 
● the bank had confirmed knowledge the consumer was traveling 
● digital delivery of dispute communications was repeatedly requested 
● physical-mail-only notice was used anyway 
The dispute was therefore closed through procedure rather than evidence. 
1) Services Not Rendered
1.1) Verified Promises vs. Verified Non-Delivery 
This dispute is a services-not-rendered matter supported by 
- documented misrepresentation 
- failure of consideration 
- total non-delivery of the merchant’s core promised deliverables 
The merchant marketed and sold a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment 
system operation that promised: 
- rapid property acquisition 
- full furnishing and setup 
- professional listing launch 
- and immediate profitability. 
These promises are not interpretation 
They are documented in the merchant’s own: 
- public advertising 
- onboarding materials 
- Invoice 
- written marketing 
- recorded sales statements 
6 

1.2) Merchant’s Promises - Verifiable Evidence 
The merchant represented that the consumer would receive: 
 • acquisition of a landlord-approved Airbnb property 
 • full interior design and furnishing 
 • professional photography 
 • automated listing launch and optimization 
 • bookings and revenue generation 
 • target profit of $3,500-$5,000+ per month per property beginning month 1
 • ability to launch a $10K+ per month Airbnb first try
 • rapid execution timeline (weeks, not months) 
These representations are verified by: 
 • merchant website screenshots advertising $10K+/month Airbnb launches 
 • merchant promotional materials stating $3.5K+ monthly average profit 
 • merchant visual timeline graphic promising rapid 90-day deployment 
 • merchant branded PDF describing DFY acquisition + setup 
 • recorded sales call transcript confirming week-by-week execution plan & profits 
 • written flyer stating: “You pay $0 if we do not find you a property.” 
 • the issued invoice documenting a fixed-fee DFY acquisition program 
 Each of these items is attached and labeled for independent verification. 
 They originate from the merchant, not the consumer. 
This establishes: 
- clear inducement terms
- performance expectations 
- reliance 
1.3) Verified Outcome After Eleven Weeks 
After more than eleven weeks from payment: 
 • no property was acquired 
 • no lease was secured 
 • no furnishing occurred 
 • no design work was completed 
 • no photography was scheduled 
 • no listing went live 
 • no bookings occurred 
7 
13300438.1.5-22

 • no revenue was generated 
 • no DFY system was deployed 
 • no promised deliverable was fulfilled 
This is not partial performance. 
This is total non-delivery of the core service purchased. 
1.4) Merchant’s Post-Payment Defense 
The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery by:
- introducing unsigned, post-payment “terms” 
- asserting retroactive hourly billing 
- presenting rules never disclosed 
- presenting documents never agreed to 
- presenting documents never signed 
The merchant also attempts to characterize delivery as:
temporary access to shared AIRDNA software costing approximately 
$34-$125/month 
This software access: 
 • expired after 3-4 weeks 
 • is a low-cost third-party tool 
 • provides inaccurate data 
 • has trivial commercial value 
 • does not replace the contracted service 
This is not performance. 
This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat a chargeback. 
1.4.A) Preparatory Activity Cannot Satisfy the Essential 
Purpose of the Contract 
The merchant may argue that access to analytics software or preliminary research constituted 
partial performance. 
Even assuming analytics research is a preparatory component of the workflow, preparatory 
steps do not equal delivery of the contracted service 
. 
The transaction was not for consulting, research assistance, or software access. 
8 

The transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment resulting in an 
operational, furnished, revenue-generating property. 
Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential purpose of the 
agreement - not by isolated preparatory activity. 
See: 
 • Restatement (Second) of Contracts §235 
 • UCC failure of consideration principles 
 • Texas contract law on essential purpose and material breach 
The essential purpose required: 
 • acquisition of a landlord-approved property 
 • furnishing and interior deployment 
 • listing activation 
 • operational launch 
 • revenue generation capability 
None of these occurred. 
Where the essential purpose of a contract fails, preparatory work cannot convert 
non-performance into performance. 
Courts consistently reject the argument that incomplete preparation satisfies a contract whose 
central objective was never achieved. 
At most, analytics access represents abandoned preliminary activity that never matured into 
delivery. 
This is legally classified as: 
 • failure of consideration 
 • material breach 
 • services not rendered 
 • unjust enrichment if payment is retained 
A party cannot retain full payment by pointing to incomplete preparation when the contracted 
result was never delivered. 
That principle applies equally in contract law and card network dispute frameworks. 
1.4.B) Token Activity Cannot Cure Total Non-Delivery 
9 
13300438.1.6-22

Providing temporary access to low-cost third-party software does not constitute delivery of a 
high-value DFY deployment system. 
This is not partial fulfillment. 
This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat chargeback eligibility. 
Mastercard dispute standards evaluate: 
- delivery of the contracted service 
- not peripheral activity 
- not unrelated tools 
- not token gestures 
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), the test is binary: 
Was the promised service delivered as agreed? 
Here: 
- no property was acquired 
- no listing was launched 
- no system was operational 
- no revenue capability existed 
Therefore: 
the contracted service was not delivered. 
Token activity cannot substitute for contractual performance. 
Allowing merchants to defeat disputes by pointing to trivial or unrelated activity would nullify 
consumer protection frameworks entirely. 
Card network rules explicitly prevent this outcome. 
1.4.C) Retention of Payment After Non-Performance 
Creates Unjust Enrichment 
If a merchant retains full payment after failing to deliver the essential purpose of the contract, 
the legal consequence is unjust enrichment. 
Unjust enrichment occurs when: 
- payment is received 
- the bargained-for benefit is not delivered 
- the recipient retains the money anyway 
Courts treat unjust enrichment as an equitable remedy independent of contract disputes. 
10 

Even absent formal contract analysis, equity requires restitution when performance fails. 
This principle reinforces chargeback eligibility. 
The consumer paid for a result. 
The result does not exist. 
Retention of funds under those circumstances is legally indefensible. 
1.5) Legal Characterization of Merchant Conduct 
Post-payment contract fabrication cannot cure non-performance. 
Token software access cannot substitute for a DFY business deployment. 
This conduct independently constitutes deceptive practice under: 
 • FTC Act §5 - unfair or deceptive acts 
 • Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act §17.46 
 • failure of consideration doctrine 
 • common law fraudulent inducement principles 
The essential purpose of the contract was never performed. 
Under contract law: 
trivial acts do not satisfy primary obligations 
1.6) Legal Classification of the Dispute 
This dispute is a textbook case of: 
 • Goods/Services Not Provided 
 • Failure of Consideration 
 • Misrepresentation 
 • Fraudulent Inducement 
 • Unenforceable Post-Payment Contract Attempt 
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 
 (Goods or Services Not Provided) 
chargeback eligibility is established when promised services are not delivered. 
The merchant’s attempt to rewrite the agreement after payment does not eliminate original 
non-delivery. 
11 
13300438.1.7-22

1.6.A) KEY LEGAL POINT: Unenforceable Post-Payment 
Terms 
A contract requires mutual assent. 
The merchant has produced no signed agreement, no acceptance record, and no 
contemporaneous disclosure showing that the consumer agreed to the “terms” now 
being asserted. 
Terms introduced after payment - or never disclosed at all - are legally 
unenforceable and cannot be used to: 
● justify non-delivery 
● convert non-performance into performance 
● defeat consumer protection rights 
Treating unilateral, unsigned merchant assertions as binding terms contradicts 
basic contract law principles and cannot support a valid dispute denial. 
Additionally, even if a merchant claims “terms exist,” the operative question is 
whether there is evidence of mutual assent at or before payment (signature, 
clickwrap acceptance record, or contemporaneous disclosure tied to the 
transaction). 
The merchant has provided no enforceable acceptance record for the post-payment 
terms now being asserted. Therefore, those terms cannot defeat a 
services-not-rendered claim or convert non-performance into performance. 
1.7) Binary Record of the Case 
The record is legally binary when evaluated against the contracted service. 
● A promised Done-For-You Airbnb deployment service exists
● Payment for that service exists 
● The contracted service was never performed 
- No property was acquired. 
12 

- No Airbnb was launched. 
- No DFY system was deployed. 
Any unrelated third-party software access referenced by the merchant does not 
constitute delivery of the contracted service and has no bearing on performance 
analysis. 
Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential 
purpose of the agreement. That essential purpose was never achieved. 
This satisfies the legal definition of: Services Not Rendered. 
1.8) Creditor Investigation Duties & Bank Liability 
Once a services-not-rendered dispute is supported by documentary evidence, the issuing bank 
is not permitted to rely solely on merchant rebuttal narratives that contradict the factual record. 
Under Regulation Z - 12 CFR §1026.13, the creditor must: 
 • conduct a reasonable investigation 
 • review submitted documentation in good faith 
 • resolve disputes based on evidence 
 • not shift the burden to the consumer once proof is submitted 
 • acknowledge disputes within 30 days 
 • resolve disputes within two billing cycles 
Repeated requests for documentation already submitted, followed by denial on a false “missing 
documentation” premise, is inconsistent with the creditor’s duty to perform a meaningful 
investigation. 
A creditor may not close a billing error dispute based on procedural technicalities when the 
evidentiary record establishes non-delivery. 
Failure to properly investigate exposes the creditor to liability under: 
 • Truth in Lending Act enforcement provisions 
 • Regulation Z dispute handling obligations 
 • Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards 
 • unfair or deceptive practices by covered entities 
 • CFPB supervisory expectations for reasonable investigations 
13 
13300438.1.8-22

1.9) Procedural Timeline Violations & Denial of 
Meaningful Notice 
This dispute was not denied on the merits. 
It was denied through a procedural structure that deprived the consumer of a meaningful 
opportunity to respond.

(As communicated by First Interstate Bank on a recorded line): 
 • Dispute formally filed: August 13, 2025 
 • Bank letter dated August 21, 2025 allegedly sent (never received) 
 • Documentation requested September 11, 2025

 • Temporary credit issued September 25, 2025

 • Response deadline November 24, 2025 (~9 days)

2.0) Response Window Defect 
The response window provided to the consumer was: 
- less than two weeks to respond 
- physical mail as the sole delivery method 
- sent while the bank had confirmed knowledge the consumer was traveling 
- refusal to provide digital delivery despite repeated requests 
- inconsistent delivery practices (personal account documents delivered digitally, dispute 
documents withheld) 
- refusal to send documentation to the verified residential address upon request 
- no supplemental notice via call, email, or secure message
The consumer never received the letter. 
No meaningful notice occurred. 
Even as of January 30, 2026, the physical correspondence has not been received. 
14 

A dispute cannot be closed for non-response when the notice structure prevents the consumer 
from receiving the notice. 
That is not procedural compliance. 
That is constructive denial of notice. 
The consumer formally requested an additional copy of the bank’s response and the merchant’s 
rebuttal documentation be mailed to the verified residential address on file. The address was 
confirmed with a bank agent on a recorded call. 
As of January 30, 2026, the requested documentation has not been received. 
2.1) Mastercard Processing Irregularity 
According to Mastercard dispute processing standards, merchant rebuttals must occur within 
structured network timelines designed to ensure fair dispute resolution. 
The merchant rebuttal in this case occurred approximately 92 days after the dispute was filed. 
The consumer was given 9 days to respond. 
This creates an extreme imbalance in procedural opportunity. 
Network dispute systems are designed to ensure: 
 • timely merchant participation 
 • reasonable consumer response opportunity 
 • equitable procedural structure 
 • good-faith processing 
Where one party is granted extended response time while the other is denied meaningful notice, 
the integrity of the dispute process is compromised. 
This is not consistent with reasonable network dispute handling. 
2.2) Creditor Knowledge of Travel Status 
The bank had actual knowledge that the consumer was traveling for work. 
This is documented by repeated calls regarding: 
- card travel authorization 
- location confirmations 
- fraud decline reversals 
- account access discussions

13300438.1.9-22

Despite this knowledge, the bank continued to rely exclusively on physical mail, despite 
repeated requests from consumer for digital delivery. (The same as how his personal account 
documents are delivered digitally with First Interstate Bank) 
This creates constructive denial of notice. 
2.3) Legal Characterization 
Closing a billing dispute based on a response window the consumer never had a practical ability 
to meet constitutes: 
 • denial of meaningful notice 
 • procedural unfairness 
 • failure of reasonable investigation 
 • creditor bad-faith handling 
 • constructive deprivation of dispute rights 
This conduct is inconsistent with: 
Regulation Z - 12 CFR §1026.13 
 requiring reasonable investigation of billing disputes 
Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards 
 prohibiting unfair or deceptive creditor practices 
Good-faith creditor obligations 
 under federal consumer credit law 
The dispute was closed through procedure - not evidence. 
2.4) Procedural Impact 
A creditor cannot close a billing dispute based on non-response when the notice method makes 
response impossible. 
That is not procedural compliance. 
That is procedural failure. 
Under federal consumer credit standards, investigations must be: 
- Reasonable 
- good-faith 
- accessible 
- not structured to guarantee consumer default 
16 

Closing a dispute on a letter the consumer never received is inconsistent with: 
- Regulation Z dispute handling duties 
- reasonable investigation standards 
- Dodd-Frank UDAAP unfairness principles 
- creditor good-faith obligations 
2.5) Merchant Timeline Irregularity 
The bank states the merchant was given 60 days to respond. 
Standard Mastercard dispute procedures do not grant merchants unlimited response time, 
particularly where services were not provided. 
The consumer was given 9 days. 
The merchant was given 60. 
This imbalance is procedurally unreasonable and materially prejudicial. 
2.6) Mastercard Compliance Obligations 
Under Mastercard dispute processing rules, the issuing bank is required to: 
 • evaluate chargeback eligibility based on delivery of services 
 • not elevate unsigned merchant terms above transaction evidence 
 • treat non-delivery as a valid reason code event 
 • prevent merchants from curing non-performance with token activity 
Where the merchant fails to deliver the contracted service, Mastercard standards require the 
dispute to proceed under: Reason Code 4853 - Goods or Services Not Provided 
Failure to properly process an eligible dispute is a network compliance issue, not a discretionary 
judgment call. 
2.7) Material Risk to the Creditor 
When a bank denies a dispute supported by documentary evidence of non-delivery, the issue is 
no longer a consumer complaint - it becomes a regulatory concern. 
Regulators evaluate patterns of:

13300438.1.10-22

 • improper dispute denials 
 • superficial investigations 
 • reliance on merchant narratives over evidence 
 • procedural closures without substantive review 
These patterns trigger supervisory review because they undermine the consumer protections 
embedded in federal credit law. 
This case presents a documented record where: 
- evidence exists 
- delivery does not exist 
- denial persists anyway

That is the definition of investigative failure. 
SECTION 3 - Quantified Regulatory Exposure & Statutory
Liability 
3.1) Truth in Lending Act / Regulation Z Civil Liability (15 
U.S.C. §1640; 12 CFR §1026.13) 
Legal anchor: Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) imposes billing error investigation and correction 
duties. The private right of action and civil liability framework is under the Truth in Lending Act 
(15 U.S.C. §1640). 
Recoverable categories in an individual consumer action may include: 
● Actual damages (fact-based harm caused by mishandling) 
● Statutory damages (subject to statutory limits in 15 U.S.C. §1640) 
● Court costs 
● Reasonable attorney’s fees (fee shifting upon prevailing) 
3.1.A) Repeated Dispute Mishandling Increases Legal Exposure (Without 
Overstating Caps) 
This matter involves multiple discrete procedural failures, including: 
18 

● improper denial despite documented non-delivery, 
● repeated “missing documentation” assertions despite submission, 
● procedural closure tied to notice that was never received, 
● appeal-level repetition of the same investigative defects. 
While statutory damages in an individual action are capped under §1640, each discrete failure 
can still support independent liability theories and increases: 
● actual damages exposure, 
● attorney-fee exposure, and 
● the reasonableness of regulatory scrutiny when conduct continues after notice. 
3.1.B) Conservative Civil Exposure Model (Defensible) 
A conservative litigation exposure model typically includes: 
● Statutory damages (capped by statute) 
● Attorney’s fees (fee shifting): commonly $25,000-$75,000+ in contested 
consumer-credit litigation (fact-dependent) 
● Costs: $2,000-$10,000 (filing, service, transcripts) 
Even conservatively, the fee-shifting reality alone can make the exposure materially exceed the 
underlying principal dispute. 
3.2) Restitution / Account Remediation Exposure 
If the dispute is corrected on the merits, the creditor must unwind the transaction effects: 
● Disputed principal: $10,500 
● Accrued interest (variable) 
● Any penalty or late fees triggered by the balance 
● Credit reporting correction (if impacted) 
This represents the clean remediation path. 
3.3) Dodd-Frank UDAAP Civil Penalty Framework 
(Regulatory context, not a consumer demand) 
19 
13300438.1.11-22

Under Dodd-Frank, regulators may impose civil money penalties for unfair or deceptive 
practices. Current inflation-adjusted tiers: 
- Tier 1: $7,217 per day 
- Tier 2 (reckless): $36,083 per day 
- Tier 3 (knowing): $1,443,275 per day 
Example 10-day enforcement proxy: 
Tier 1 → $72,170 
 Tier 2 → $360,830 
 Tier 3 → $14,432,750 
This illustrates supervisory exposure environment, not a private claim. 
3.3.A) Escalation Risk When Conduct Becomes Reckless 
UDAAP tiers escalate based on mental state. 
Once a creditor continues improper handling after notice, regulators evaluate conduct under 
reckless or knowing tiers. 
Patterns that trigger Tier 2 review include: 
● repeated denial after notice 
● ignoring documentary evidence 
● procedural closure without investigation 
Example 5-day Tier 2 window: 
$36,083 × 5 = $180,415 
This demonstrates why dispute handling is compliance-critical. 
3.4) Merchant-Side Deceptive Practices Exposure 
(Context) 
If the merchant’s inducement claims and post-payment “term” assertions are proven deceptive, 
the merchant may face liability under applicable state consumer protection and common-law 
fraud principles. 
This is included as context because it reinforces a core dispute fact: a merchant cannot retain 
payment for an undelivered service by retroactively asserting unsigned terms, and the

bank should not treat unilateral, post-payment assertions as binding contractual terms where 
there is no mutual assent.

- Discovery / evidence handling: $5,000 - $25,000 
- Depositions / expert review: $5,000 - $20,000 
Total practical litigation environment: $35,000 - $145,000+ independent of the $10,500 principal.

● denies a supported billing error dispute,

5.2) ECOA Non-Retaliation & Anti-Discrimination Notice 
Federal law expressly prohibits retaliation or adverse action against a consumer for asserting 
protected rights. 
Under the Equal Credit Opportunity Act (ECOA), 15 U.S.C. §1691, and implementing 
Regulation B: 
● A creditor may not discriminate against or penalize a consumer 
 for exercising rights under consumer credit protection laws. 
● Adverse treatment linked to dispute activity, complaints, or enforcement efforts is 
prohibited. 
Accordingly, I formally request and expect written confirmation that: 
● This dispute and its resolution will not be reported or characterized negatively 
● No adverse action, account limitation, repricing, or relationship impairment 
 will occur as a result of asserting my lawful dispute rights
● My standing and relationship with First Interstate Bank will remain intact 
This request is consistent with ECOA, UDAAP principles, and CFPB supervisory expectations.

Accusatory) 
Under Dodd-Frank’s UDAAP framework, conduct may be deemed unfair when it: 
● causes substantial consumer injury, 
● is not reasonably avoidable by the consumer, 
● and is not outweighed by countervailing benefits. 
Requiring a traveling consumer to respond to physical mail only, 
 - while refusing digital delivery,

 - despite confirmed knowledge of travel status, 
 - and then closing a dispute for “non-response,” 
creates precisely the type of avoidable consumer harm UDAAP is designed to prevent. 
This is especially true when the underlying transaction involves: 
● documented non-delivery, 
● partial refund admissions by the merchant, 
● and reliance on unsigned post-payment terms. 
I raise this not to accuse, but to underscore why prompt corrective action is the lowest-risk and 
most compliant outcome.

● confirming non-retaliation and account integrity 
then no regulatory filings are necessary. 
Resolution at this stage benefits all parties and avoids unnecessary cost, scrutiny, and 
administrative burden. 
5.5) Plain-English Closing (Layperson, High-Impact) 
I paid for a service that was never delivered. 
The merchant admitted this by partially refunding me. 
The remaining balance is still being withheld. 
I provided overwhelming proof. 
28 

I followed every instruction. 
I never received the letters I was supposedly required to respond to. 
The bank relied on unsigned terms I never agreed to and closed my case through 
procedure, not facts. 
I am confident that, if reviewed by a court or regulator, the evidence and the law 
would be squarely in my favor. 
I do not want conflict. I want fairness. 
I am therefore requesting immediate correction of this dispute, a refund of the 
remaining $10,500 plus interest, and a good-faith resolution of the additional losses 
caused by this process. 
I am prepared to defend my rights if necessary, but I sincerely hope we can resolve 
this amicably and preserve a positive relationship. 
6.1) EXHIBIT APPENDIX 
Appendix A - Evidentiary Exhibits 
Each exhibit referenced below is attached, labeled, and originates from either the merchant, the 
bank, or contemporaneous communications. No exhibit relies on post-hoc interpretation or 
inference. 
Exhibit A - Invoice & Payment Proof 
Purpose: Establishes the original transaction and consideration. 
● Merchant-issued invoice reflecting $18,000 for a fixed-fee Done-For-You Airbnb 
acquisition and deployment program. 
● Proof of payment confirming the transaction occurred. 
Relevance: 
 Establishes the baseline transaction from which all refund, non-delivery, and dispute issues 
arise. 
Exhibit B - Merchant Advertising & Public Representations 
29 
13300438.1.16-22

Purpose: Establishes inducement, promises, and performance expectations. 
Includes merchant-created materials such as: 
● Website and promotional representations advertising $10,000+ monthly Airbnb 
performance. 
● Marketing statements representing $3,500–$5,000+ monthly profit per property. 
● Visual timeline materials promising rapid execution and deployment. 
● Written representations describing a Done-For-You acquisition, furnishing, and 
launch system. 
Relevance: 
 These materials define the essential purpose of the contract and the promised outcome, 
independent of any later “terms.” 
Exhibit C - Sales Call Evidence 
Purpose: Confirms oral inducement and reliance. 
● Recorded sales call transcript/text reflecting: 
○ Done-For-You structure 
○ Operational delivery promises 
○ Timeline expectations 
○ Profit and performance representations 
○ Deployment assurances 
Relevance: 
 Corroborates Exhibit B and demonstrates reliance on merchant representations at the time of 
sale. 
Exhibit D - Cancellation & Refund Communications (Texts and Emails) 
Purpose: Establishes non-delivery, cancellation, and refund acknowledgment. 
Includes: 
● Text and email communications confirming missed timelines and non-delivery. 
● Merchant acknowledgment of failure to deliver. 
● Written confirmation of a $7,500 refund. 
30 

● Confirmation that $10,500 remains retained by the merchant. 
● Merchant’s post-payment attempts to justify retention using non-core activity. 
Relevance: 
 This exhibit definitively establishes: 
● Non-performance 
● Partial refund acknowledgment 
● Remaining disputed amount 
● Post-hoc justification attempts 
Exhibit E - Initial Dispute Packet Submitted to First Interstate Bank 
Purpose: Establishes consumer compliance and evidentiary submission. 
● Full dispute documentation packet submitted on September 5, 2025. 
● Includes: 
○ Services-not-rendered explanation 
○ Timeline narrative 
○ Merchant representations 
○ Cancellation and refund demands 
○ Supporting exhibits 
Relevance: 
 Demonstrates that the bank received complete documentation well before any alleged 
“non-response” determination. 
Exhibit F - Fax Transmission to First Interstate Bank 
Purpose: Establishes proof of delivery channel and submission effort. 
● Fax confirmation showing transmission of dispute-related documentation to First 
Interstate Bank. 
Relevance: 
 Directly contradicts any assertion that documentation was not provided or not received. 
Exhibit G - Prior Executive Summary Submission 
31 
13300438.1.17-22

Purpose: Establishes consistency and escalation history. 
● Prior executive-level summary submitted to the bank outlining: 
○ Services-not-rendered basis 
○ Non-delivery facts 
○ Procedural concerns 
○ Request for correction 
Relevance: 
 Shows repeated, good-faith efforts to resolve the dispute based on evidence — not procedure. 
7.1) Important Clarification Regarding Bank 
Correspondence 
The consumer did not receive the physical letter allegedly mailed by the bank in November 
2025. 
 No exhibit purports to include that letter. 
All references to deadlines and procedural closure are based on: 
● Information communicated by bank representatives on recorded calls 
● The bank’s subsequent actions 
● The absence of any received written notice 
This distinction is intentional and accurate. 
8.1) Closing Evidentiary Integrity Statement 
All exhibits: 
● Are contemporaneous 
● Originate from the merchant or the bank 
● Were created prior to or during the dispute 
● Are not reconstructed or speculative 
The evidentiary record establishes: 
● A promised Done-For-You (DFY) service 
● Payment made 
32 

● Non-delivery of the essential purpose 
● Partial refund acknowledgment 
● Continued retention of $10,500 without performance 
● Procedural handling defects by the issuing bank 
8.2) PLAIN-ENGLISH CLOSING STATEMENT & 
SETTLEMENT POSITION - Good-Faith Resolution 
Request 
I am not seeking conflict. 
I am seeking a lawful resolution based on facts, documentation, and established 
consumer protection standards. 
While First Interstate Bank has suggested that this matter may fall outside internal 
chargeback timelines, I am confident that: 
● the merchant’s failure to deliver the promised service 
● the reliance on unsigned, unenforceable “terms” 
● and the bank’s repeated denial despite full documentation 
would not withstand judicial or regulatory scrutiny. 
That said, I prefer to resolve this matter amicably and preserve a positive 
relationship with First Interstate Bank. 
Accordingly, I am requesting the following resolution: 
1. Immediate refund of the remaining $10,500 
2. Reversal of all interest and fees associated with the disputed amount 
3. Written confirmation that this dispute - and my exercise of consumer 
protection rights - will not be used adversely against my account, credit 
standing, or relationship with the bank 
I am not requesting punitive action. 
33 
13300438.1.18-22

I am requesting that the bank correct a documented error and reverse a transaction 
that was never legally earned by the merchant. 
If this matter cannot be resolved at this level, I am prepared to protect my rights 
through formal legal and regulatory channels using the extensive, undisputed 
documentation attached and more. 
I hope that will not be necessary and that this can be resolved promptly and 
professionally. 
Sincerely,

Yes / No Compliance Clarification - Purpose Statement
The following questions are presented to establish a clear, factual record 
regarding First Interstate Bank’s handling of my billing dispute. 
Each question is intentionally framed to be answerable yes or no, based solely 
on objective facts, records, and actions taken by the bank. These questions are 
designed to clarify: 
● what evidence was received, 
● what investigation steps were taken, 
● what notice was provided, 
● and whether statutory and regulatory duties were satisfied. 
These questions are not argumentative. 
They are intended to identify undisputed facts, procedural compliance, and 
decision logic underlying the bank’s denial and closure of my dispute. 
Any inability to answer “yes” to a question that reflects a required consumer 
protection obligation is relevant to CFPB review, Regulation Z compliance, and 
UDAAP analysis. 
 YES / NO ADMISSION SERIES 
A. Services Not Rendered – Objective Facts 
1. Yes or No: Did the merchant ever deliver a completed, operational Airbnb property as 
promised in the transaction? 
2. Yes or No: Did the merchant ever acquire a lease or property on my behalf? 
3. Yes or No: Did any Airbnb listing ever go live as part of the paid Done-For-You service? 
13300438.1.19-22

4. Yes or No: Was any revenue ever generated from the promised service? 
5. Yes or No: Did the merchant issue a partial refund, acknowledging non-delivery of 
services? 
(If “yes” to #5, non-delivery is legally conceded.) 
B. Contract & Terms Validity 
6. Yes or No: Did the bank receive any signed contract showing my agreement to the 
post-payment terms cited in the denial? 
7. Yes or No: Did the bank receive any clickwrap or electronic acceptance record
proving assent to those terms before payment? 
8. Yes or No: Were the terms relied upon in the denial disclosed prior to or at the time of 
payment? 
9. Yes or No: Does the bank agree that unsigned, post-payment terms are not 
enforceable contracts? 
C. Investigation Duties (Regulation Z) 
10. Yes or No: Did the bank receive my complete dispute documentation on September 5, 
2025? 
11. Yes or No: Did the bank acknowledge receipt of that documentation? 
12. Yes or No: Did the bank deny the dispute citing “missing documentation” despite having 
received it? 
13. Yes or No: Did the bank rely primarily on the merchant’s rebuttal narrative rather than 
the documentary evidence? 
14. Yes or No: Did the bank independently verify whether the contracted service was 
actually delivered? 

D. Notice & Procedural Fairness 
15. Yes or No: Did the bank rely exclusively on physical mail to deliver the response 
deadline notice? 
16. Yes or No: Was the bank aware that I was traveling at the time the notice was allegedly 
sent? 
17. Yes or No: Did I request digital delivery of dispute correspondence?
18. Yes or No: Did the bank refuse to provide digital delivery despite using digital delivery 
for other account communications? 
19. Yes or No: Has the bank confirmed that I never received the November 2025 letter? 
E. Timing & Disparity 
20. Yes or No: Was the merchant given approximately 60+ days to respond to the dispute? 
21. Yes or No: Was I given 9 days or fewer to respond to the merchant rebuttal? 
22. Yes or No: Would the bank agree this created a procedural imbalance? 
F. Outcome Logic 
23. Yes or No: Is it the bank’s position that temporary access to low-cost third-party 
software constitutes delivery of a high-value DFY service? 
24. Yes or No: Does the bank contend that preparatory or token activity cures total 
non-delivery? 
25. Yes or No: Would the bank agree that allowing such logic would undermine consumer 
dispute protections? 
G. Regulatory Exposure Awareness 
13300438.1.20-22

26. Yes or No: Is the bank aware that closing a dispute without meaningful notice may 
violate Regulation Z? 
27. Yes or No: Is the bank aware that such conduct may constitute UDAAP under 
Dodd-Frank? 
28. Yes or No: Is the bank aware that retaliation or adverse treatment following a dispute is 
prohibited under ECOA? 
H. Resolution Capability 
29. Yes or No: Does the bank have the authority to correct this dispute administratively? 
30. Yes or No: Would reversing the charge fully resolve this matter without litigation or 
regulatory escalation? 
I. Additional Questions 
31. Mastercard / Reason Code Admission
Yes or No: Did the bank evaluate this dispute under Mastercard Reason Code 
4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided)? 
32. Receipt vs. Review Distinction 
Banks often claim “received” but not “reviewed.” 
Add under C. Investigation Duties: 
Yes or No: Did the bank substantively review the evidence demonstrating 
non-delivery before denying the dispute? 
33. Notice Confirmation

Yes or No: Did the bank confirm delivery or receipt of the November 2025 letter 
before closing the dispute? 
34. Authority + Refusal
Yes or No: Has the bank chosen not to exercise that authority despite evidence of 
non-delivery? 
J. Enforcement of Unenforceable Merchant Terms 
35. Yes or No: Did the bank rely on a merchant-submitted agreement or terms document that 
was unsigned by the consumer when denying the dispute? 
36. Yes or No: Did the bank treat that unsigned document as binding contractual authority? 
37. Yes or No: Did the bank deny or uphold the dispute based, in whole or in part, on those 
unsigned terms? 
38. Yes or No: Did the bank verify whether those terms were ever presented to or agreed to 
by the consumer prior to payment? 
39. Yes or No: Did the bank consider whether enforcing unsigned, post-payment terms is 
legally permissible under basic contract law principles? 
These questions force an admission that the bank substituted merchant assertions for lawful 
agreement. 
K. Appeals & Repeated Denial After Notice 
40. Yes or No: Did the consumer appeal the dispute after the initial denial? 
41. Yes or No: Did the consumer specifically raise the issue that the merchant’s relied-upon 
terms were unsigned and unenforceable during the appeal? 
13300438.1.21-22

42. Yes or No: Did the bank deny or uphold the dispute on appeal without addressing the 
lack of a signed agreement? 
43. Yes or No: Did the bank continue to rule in the merchant’s favor after being directly 
notified of this defect? 
L. Knowledge of Merchant’s Representations & 
Inducement 
44. Yes or No: Was the bank aware that the merchant publicly advertised Done-For-You 
Airbnb services promising $10,000+ per month performance? 
45. Yes or No: Was the bank aware that the merchant represented expected profits of 
approximately $3,500+ per month per property? 
46. Yes or No: Did the bank review or receive documentation of these representations as part 
of the dispute evidence? 
47. Yes or No: Did the bank consider whether the merchant’s failure to deliver any property, 
listing, or revenue constituted non-delivery of the essential promised service? 
48. Yes or No: Did the bank nonetheless rule in the merchant’s favor despite these 
representations and the absence of delivery? 
M. Continued Enforcement After Knowledge 
49. Yes or No: After receiving notice of non-delivery, unenforceable terms, and procedural 
defects, did the bank take any corrective action before closing the dispute? 
50. Yes or No: Did the bank escalate the dispute for supervisory or compliance review after 
these issues were raised?

YOUR COMPLAINT
On August 13, 2025, I filed a credit card billing dispute with First
Interstate Bank for $10,500 related to a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb
service that was never delivered. I paid $18,000 for a fixed-fee service
promising property acquisition, furnishing, Airbnb listing launch, and
revenue generation. After 11+ weeks, no property, no listing, and no
revenue existed. The merchant acknowledged non-delivery by issuing a
$7,500 partial refund, leaving $10,500 outstanding. I submitted complete
dispute documentation on September 5, 2025. Despite this, the bank:
asserted “missing documentation” after receipt, relied on the merchant’s
rebuttal instead of verifying delivery, and denied the dispute and appeal
without addressing non-delivery. The bank later closed the dispute for
“non-response” based on a physical letter I never received, despite
knowing I was traveling and despite my repeated requests for digital
delivery. No alternative notice was provided. The bank also relied on
unsigned, post-payment merchant terms that I never agreed to. There is no
evidence of assent. Such terms cannot cure non-delivery. These actions

ADDITIONAL COMMENTS
Supplemental Statement - Timeline Clarification & Regulatory Compliance
(Hybrid Addendum) First Interstate Bank’s CFPB response relies on
generalized references to “timeframes” while refusing to disclose the actual
dates necessary to verify compliance with federal law and card-network
standards. The undisputed timeline is as follows: Aug 13, 2025 - Billing
dispute opened Sept 5, 2025 - Complete dispute documentation submitted
Sept 25, 2025 - Provisional credit issued Nov 13, 2025 - Merchant rebuttal
dated Nov 14, 2025 - Bank claims notice mailed (never received) Nov 24,
2025 - Alleged response deadline This reflects approximately 92 days from
dispute initiation to the merchant rebuttal date. Despite this, the consumer
was allegedly given ~9 days to respond, via physical mail only, during a
period when the bank had confirmed knowledge that the consumer was
traveling and had repeatedly requested digital delivery. First Interstate
Bank has not disclosed: the date the merchant rebuttal was received, the
date it was processed, or the date it was reviewed. Without these dates,
neither the consumer nor the CFPB can verify compliance with: Regulation
Z (12 CFR §1026.13) - reasonable investigation and meaningful notice
requirements, Mastercard dispute processing standards for Goods or
Services Not Provided, or Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards, which prohibit
unfair procedural structures that cause avoidable consumer harm. Further,
the bank continues to rely on a “consultation = fulfillment” theory and
unsigned, post-payment merchant terms while ignoring undisputed facts:
no property was acquired, no Airbnb listing went live, no revenue was
generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging nondelivery. Closing a dispute under these circumstances reflects procedural
denial rather than an evidence-based determination. I am requesting:
Disclosure of the exact receipt, processing, and review dates for the
merchant rebuttal; and Reconsideration of the dispute based on
documented non-delivery, not unsigned merchant assertions. Absent
correction, this matter presents ongoing Reg Z and UDAAP compliance
concerns appropriate for supervisory review.

YOUR COMPLAINT
Regulation Z & UDAAP Noncompliance - Failure to Investigate and Ongoing Violations This complaint concerns
First Interstate Bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation, and its willful misconduct, willful negligence,
reckless disregard, and willful noncompliance with Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) and Dodd-Frank UDAAP
standards in handling my credit card billing dispute. This submission is not a duplicate of CFPB Case 260131-
28391819. That complaint addressed the underlying transaction. This complaint addresses the bank’s procedural
and substantive violations during and after the dispute process. First Interstate Bank denied a services-notrendered dispute without identifying any evidence that the contracted service was delivered. No property was
delivered, no listing launched, no revenue generated, and the merchant issued a partial refund acknowledging nondelivery. Despite this undisputed record, the bank denied the dispute and continues to charge interest. The bank
relied on unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and
refused to disclose the dates the rebuttal was received, processed, or reviewed, preventing verification of
compliance with Regulation Z. The bank gave the merchant approximately 60-90+ days to respond (Aug 13, 2025
to Nov 13, 2025), while giving me fewer than 10 days, by physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and
after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was
closed anyway. The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines have passed. This is legally
false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure when the investigation
is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies
merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or
consultation does not constitute delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof
provided on the subject in detail, directly from the merchant themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853,
the test is binary: Was the promised service delivered? Here, the answer is no. The promised service with provided
proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb acquired property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits
$3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or preparatory activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No
timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank continues to demonstrate willful negligence, and
routinely ignores action relating to directly addressed compliance with UDAAP, REG Z, proper investigation, etc.
They refuse to answer compliance related questions, and continue to violate consumer chargeback rights, and are
abusing their powers as a bank, due to a lack of due diligence, and repeated documented willful misconduct. I am
requesting immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP
violations persist after notice and appeal, warranting CFPB oversight. This must be escalated to all relevant
enforcement agencies.

unsigned, post-payment merchant terms, refused to provide the merchant’s rebuttal documentation, and refused to disclose the

physical mail only, despite knowing I was traveling and after I requested digital delivery. I have never received the alleged
November 14, 2025 letter, yet the dispute was closed anyway. The bank now claims it “cannot assist” because internal timelines
have passed. This is legally false. Regulation Z provides no safe harbor for denying a billing error based on procedure when the
investigation is unreasonable or when the underlying facts establish non-delivery. A procedurally timely denial that ratifies
merchant non-performance is not compliance - it is facilitation. Temporary access to: AirDNA, marketplaces, or consultation does
not constitute delivery of the promised DFY merchant services with an abundance of proof provided on the subject in detail,
directly from the merchant themselves. Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853, the test is binary: Was the promised service
delivered? Here, the answer is no. The promised service with provided proof explicitly states a DFY (Done-For-You) Airbnb
acquired property; designed, furnished, & listed that profits $3,500-$5,000+/mo after operational expenses. Token or preparatory
activity cannot cure total non-delivery. No timeline argument can cure that defect. First Interstate Bank continues to demonstrate

immediate supervisory review and administrative correction. These Regulation Z and UDAAP violations persist after notice and
appeal, warranting CFPB oversight. This must be escalated to all relevant enforcement agencies.
What would be a fair resolution to this issue?
