ADDITIONAL COMMENTS
CFPB Case 260217-28952925 - Consumer Feedback The issuer upheld denial of a billing error dispute while
refusing to produce the documentation it relied upon, closing the dispute without proof of proper notice, and
making a factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records that may require verification. Despite
repeated direct requests, no executed agreement, no merchant representment packet, and no proof of mailing of the
November 14 letter has been produced. These deficiencies prevent independent verification of investigation
adequacy under 12 CFR §1026.13. The materials identified below have been requested multiple times directly from
the bank. The bank has refused or failed to produce them. 1. Executed Governing Agreement Not Produced The
denial relies on alleged merchant terms including hourly billing, non-refundability, and modified service
characterization. Despite repeated written requests, the bank has not produced: - Any signed governing agreement -
Any signed authorization for hourly billing - Any executed agreement establishing non-refundable terms - Any
document evidencing assent to modified deliverables If the issuer cannot produce an executed agreement supporting
the terms relied upon, the investigation cannot be independently verified as reasonable under 12 CFR §1026.13. An
investigation that relies on unsigned or unexecuted merchant assertions is not independently supportable without
documentary proof. 2. Merchant Representment Packet Withheld After Repeated Requests The bank states the
merchant responded on October 30, 2025 and that it relied upon those materials in denying the dispute. I have
repeatedly requested: - The complete merchant representment packet - All attachments referenced - The full internal
dispute case log with date/time stamps These materials have not been produced. Regulation Z requires that, upon
request, the issuer provide copies of documents relied upon in making its determination. Continued refusal or
failure to provide the documentation relied upon prevents meaningful review of the investigation. If the issuer
cannot produce the documentation it relied upon, the denial lacks a verifiable evidentiary basis. 3. November 14,
2025 Letter - No Proof of Mailing or Delivery The bank asserts a “Need More Information” letter was mailed
November 14, 2025 and that the dispute was closed due to non-response. I have repeatedly requested documented
proof of mailing and delivery. None has been provided. The bank has not produced: - Proof of mailing method -
Tracking number - Certified mail receipt - Proof of delivery or returned mail - Copy of envelope and address used
Closing a dispute for non-response without proof that notice was properly mailed and delivered deprives the
consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond. If proof of mailing and delivery cannot be produced, the
procedural basis for closing the dispute is not independently supportable. 4. Statement Regarding Travel Notices -
Verification Required The bank asserts it has “no record of a travel notice.” I called multiple times on recorded lines
to clear fraud flags while traveling and notified the bank of my travel states on multiple occasions. Because the
issuer has made a factual representation to the CFPB regarding account records, preservation and review of: - All
call recordings - Fraud department notes - Internal account annotations - Travel-notice logs is necessary to verify
the accuracy of statements made in this response. If internal recordings or logs reflect travel notifications, the
statement made to the CFPB would be materially inaccurate. 5. Investigation Adequacy Under 12 CFR §1026.13
The denial appears based on merchant narrative rather than documented proof of agreed contractual terms or actual
delivery of the promised service. Without production of: - The executed governing agreement - The documentation
relied upon in denial - The complete representment file - The dispute case log with timestamps Proof of proper
notice the reasonableness of the investigation cannot be independently evaluated. Once these deficiencies were
raised and documentation was repeatedly requested, continued refusal to produce the materials relied upon raises a
material question regarding compliance with 12 CFR §1026.13. Requested CFPB Action I respectfully request that
the CFPB require the issuer to produce: - Signed governing agreement relied upon - Signed hourly billing
authorization (if claimed) - Complete merchant representment packet with attachments - Complete dispute case log

Consumer:

Purpose of Submission:
Formal dispute correction request, compliance notice, and good-faith settlement 
proposal regarding a services-not-rendered transaction supported by documentary 
evidence.

Plain Statement of the Issue 
This matter is straightforward. 
I paid a merchant for a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment service that was 
clearly promised, clearly defined, and never delivered. 
The merchant accepted payment, failed to deliver the promised services, missed all agreed 
timelines, and later attempted to justify non-delivery by asserting “terms” and billing concepts 
that were never presented, never agreed to, and never signed by me. 
Those post-payment terms are unenforceable as a matter of law and cannot be used to 
defeat a services-not-rendered claim. 
Despite this, First Interstate Bank has treated the merchant’s unilateral, unsigned assertions as 
if they were governing contractual terms - even though no valid agreement exists reflecting 
those provisions. 
I have attached extensive, undeniable documentation originating from the merchant itself - 
including advertising, invoices, sales representations, written communications, and recorded 
statements - all of which establish: 
● what was promised 
● that payment was made 
● that delivery never occurred 
This is not a quality dispute or a misunderstanding. 
This is a documented services-not-rendered dispute involving misrepresentation and fraudulent 
inducement. 
Under consumer protection law and Mastercard dispute standards, a merchant cannot cure 
non-delivery by inventing or retroactively enforcing terms that were never agreed to, and a bank 
cannot rely on such terms to deny a valid dispute. 
I am requesting that First Interstate Bank correct this matter immediately by refunding the 
remaining $10,500, plus all associated interest and fees, consistent with federal consumer credit 
law and network dispute rules.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
This submission concerns a billing dispute arising from a paid transaction for a Done-For-You 
Airbnb acquisition and deployment program that was never delivered. 
This dispute is not ambiguous. It is not a quality complaint. It is not a misunderstanding. It is a 
documented services-not-rendered event supported by evidence of: 
● misrepresentation 
● failure of consideration 
● total non-performance 
The merchant accepted payment for a fixed-fee program promising: 
● property acquisition 
● furnishing and deployment 
● professional listing launch 
● immediate profitability 
After more than eleven weeks: 
● no property was acquired 
● no deployment occurred 
● no revenue-producing system existed 
The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery through unsigned post-payment terms and 
token activity unrelated to the essential purpose of the contract. That conduct cannot legally 
cure non-performance. 
The evidentiary record is binary: 
● promised service exists 
● payment exists 
● delivery does not exist 
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), this satisfies 
chargeback eligibility. 
Despite this, the issuing bank denied the dispute and subsequent appeals under a false premise 
that documentation was missing, even though the complete documentation packet had already 
been submitted and confirmed. 
5 
13300438.1.4-22

The dispute was ultimately closed based on an alleged non-response tied to a physical letter the 
consumer never received, during a period when: 
● the bank had confirmed knowledge the consumer was traveling 
● digital delivery of dispute communications was repeatedly requested 
● physical-mail-only notice was used anyway 
The dispute was therefore closed through procedure rather than evidence. 
1) Services Not Rendered
1.1) Verified Promises vs. Verified Non-Delivery 
This dispute is a services-not-rendered matter supported by 
- documented misrepresentation 
- failure of consideration 
- total non-delivery of the merchant’s core promised deliverables 
The merchant marketed and sold a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment 
system operation that promised: 
- rapid property acquisition 
- full furnishing and setup 
- professional listing launch 
- and immediate profitability. 
These promises are not interpretation 
They are documented in the merchant’s own: 
- public advertising 
- onboarding materials 
- Invoice 
- written marketing 
- recorded sales statements 
6 

1.2) Merchant’s Promises - Verifiable Evidence 
The merchant represented that the consumer would receive: 
 • acquisition of a landlord-approved Airbnb property 
 • full interior design and furnishing 
 • professional photography 
 • automated listing launch and optimization 
 • bookings and revenue generation 
 • target profit of $3,500-$5,000+ per month per property beginning month 1
 • ability to launch a $10K+ per month Airbnb first try
 • rapid execution timeline (weeks, not months) 
These representations are verified by: 
 • merchant website screenshots advertising $10K+/month Airbnb launches 
 • merchant promotional materials stating $3.5K+ monthly average profit 
 • merchant visual timeline graphic promising rapid 90-day deployment 
 • merchant branded PDF describing DFY acquisition + setup 
 • recorded sales call transcript confirming week-by-week execution plan & profits 
 • written flyer stating: “You pay $0 if we do not find you a property.” 
 • the issued invoice documenting a fixed-fee DFY acquisition program 
 Each of these items is attached and labeled for independent verification. 
 They originate from the merchant, not the consumer. 
This establishes: 
- clear inducement terms
- performance expectations 
- reliance 
1.3) Verified Outcome After Eleven Weeks 
After more than eleven weeks from payment: 
 • no property was acquired 
 • no lease was secured 
 • no furnishing occurred 
 • no design work was completed 
 • no photography was scheduled 
 • no listing went live 
 • no bookings occurred 
7 
13300438.1.5-22

 • no revenue was generated 
 • no DFY system was deployed 
 • no promised deliverable was fulfilled 
This is not partial performance. 
This is total non-delivery of the core service purchased. 
1.4) Merchant’s Post-Payment Defense 
The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery by:
- introducing unsigned, post-payment “terms” 
- asserting retroactive hourly billing 
- presenting rules never disclosed 
- presenting documents never agreed to 
- presenting documents never signed 
The merchant also attempts to characterize delivery as:
temporary access to shared AIRDNA software costing approximately 
$34-$125/month 
This software access: 
 • expired after 3-4 weeks 
 • is a low-cost third-party tool 
 • provides inaccurate data 
 • has trivial commercial value 
 • does not replace the contracted service 
This is not performance. 
This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat a chargeback. 
1.4.A) Preparatory Activity Cannot Satisfy the Essential 
Purpose of the Contract 
The merchant may argue that access to analytics software or preliminary research constituted 
partial performance. 
Even assuming analytics research is a preparatory component of the workflow, preparatory 
steps do not equal delivery of the contracted service 
. 
The transaction was not for consulting, research assistance, or software access. 
8 

The transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment resulting in an 
operational, furnished, revenue-generating property. 
Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential purpose of the 
agreement - not by isolated preparatory activity. 
See: 
 • Restatement (Second) of Contracts §235 
 • UCC failure of consideration principles 
 • Texas contract law on essential purpose and material breach 
The essential purpose required: 
 • acquisition of a landlord-approved property 
 • furnishing and interior deployment 
 • listing activation 
 • operational launch 
 • revenue generation capability 
None of these occurred. 
Where the essential purpose of a contract fails, preparatory work cannot convert 
non-performance into performance. 
Courts consistently reject the argument that incomplete preparation satisfies a contract whose 
central objective was never achieved. 
At most, analytics access represents abandoned preliminary activity that never matured into 
delivery. 
This is legally classified as: 
 • failure of consideration 
 • material breach 
 • services not rendered 
 • unjust enrichment if payment is retained 
A party cannot retain full payment by pointing to incomplete preparation when the contracted 
result was never delivered. 
That principle applies equally in contract law and card network dispute frameworks. 
1.4.B) Token Activity Cannot Cure Total Non-Delivery 
9 
13300438.1.6-22

Providing temporary access to low-cost third-party software does not constitute delivery of a 
high-value DFY deployment system. 
This is not partial fulfillment. 
This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat chargeback eligibility. 
Mastercard dispute standards evaluate: 
- delivery of the contracted service 
- not peripheral activity 
- not unrelated tools 
- not token gestures 
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), the test is binary: 
Was the promised service delivered as agreed? 
Here: 
- no property was acquired 
- no listing was launched 
- no system was operational 
- no revenue capability existed 
Therefore: 
the contracted service was not delivered. 
Token activity cannot substitute for contractual performance. 
Allowing merchants to defeat disputes by pointing to trivial or unrelated activity would nullify 
consumer protection frameworks entirely. 
Card network rules explicitly prevent this outcome. 
1.4.C) Retention of Payment After Non-Performance 
Creates Unjust Enrichment 
If a merchant retains full payment after failing to deliver the essential purpose of the contract, 
the legal consequence is unjust enrichment. 
Unjust enrichment occurs when: 
- payment is received 
- the bargained-for benefit is not delivered 
- the recipient retains the money anyway 
Courts treat unjust enrichment as an equitable remedy independent of contract disputes. 
10 

Even absent formal contract analysis, equity requires restitution when performance fails. 
This principle reinforces chargeback eligibility. 
The consumer paid for a result. 
The result does not exist. 
Retention of funds under those circumstances is legally indefensible. 
1.5) Legal Characterization of Merchant Conduct 
Post-payment contract fabrication cannot cure non-performance. 
Token software access cannot substitute for a DFY business deployment. 
This conduct independently constitutes deceptive practice under: 
 • FTC Act §5 - unfair or deceptive acts 
 • Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act §17.46 
 • failure of consideration doctrine 
 • common law fraudulent inducement principles 
The essential purpose of the contract was never performed. 
Under contract law: 
trivial acts do not satisfy primary obligations 
1.6) Legal Classification of the Dispute 
This dispute is a textbook case of: 
 • Goods/Services Not Provided 
 • Failure of Consideration 
 • Misrepresentation 
 • Fraudulent Inducement 
 • Unenforceable Post-Payment Contract Attempt 
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 
 (Goods or Services Not Provided) 
chargeback eligibility is established when promised services are not delivered. 
The merchant’s attempt to rewrite the agreement after payment does not eliminate original 
non-delivery. 
11 
13300438.1.7-22

1.6.A) KEY LEGAL POINT: Unenforceable Post-Payment 
Terms 
A contract requires mutual assent. 
The merchant has produced no signed agreement, no acceptance record, and no 
contemporaneous disclosure showing that the consumer agreed to the “terms” now 
being asserted. 
Terms introduced after payment - or never disclosed at all - are legally 
unenforceable and cannot be used to: 
● justify non-delivery 
● convert non-performance into performance 
● defeat consumer protection rights 
Treating unilateral, unsigned merchant assertions as binding terms contradicts 
basic contract law principles and cannot support a valid dispute denial. 
Additionally, even if a merchant claims “terms exist,” the operative question is 
whether there is evidence of mutual assent at or before payment (signature, 
clickwrap acceptance record, or contemporaneous disclosure tied to the 
transaction). 
The merchant has provided no enforceable acceptance record for the post-payment 
terms now being asserted. Therefore, those terms cannot defeat a 
services-not-rendered claim or convert non-performance into performance. 
1.7) Binary Record of the Case 
The record is legally binary when evaluated against the contracted service. 
● A promised Done-For-You Airbnb deployment service exists
● Payment for that service exists 
● The contracted service was never performed 
- No property was acquired. 
12 

- No Airbnb was launched. 
- No DFY system was deployed. 
Any unrelated third-party software access referenced by the merchant does not 
constitute delivery of the contracted service and has no bearing on performance 
analysis. 
Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential 
purpose of the agreement. That essential purpose was never achieved. 
This satisfies the legal definition of: Services Not Rendered. 
1.8) Creditor Investigation Duties & Bank Liability 
Once a services-not-rendered dispute is supported by documentary evidence, the issuing bank 
is not permitted to rely solely on merchant rebuttal narratives that contradict the factual record. 
Under Regulation Z - 12 CFR §1026.13, the creditor must: 
 • conduct a reasonable investigation 
 • review submitted documentation in good faith 
 • resolve disputes based on evidence 
 • not shift the burden to the consumer once proof is submitted 
 • acknowledge disputes within 30 days 
 • resolve disputes within two billing cycles 
Repeated requests for documentation already submitted, followed by denial on a false “missing 
documentation” premise, is inconsistent with the creditor’s duty to perform a meaningful 
investigation. 
A creditor may not close a billing error dispute based on procedural technicalities when the 
evidentiary record establishes non-delivery. 
Failure to properly investigate exposes the creditor to liability under: 
 • Truth in Lending Act enforcement provisions 
 • Regulation Z dispute handling obligations 
 • Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards 
 • unfair or deceptive practices by covered entities 
 • CFPB supervisory expectations for reasonable investigations 
13 
13300438.1.8-22

1.9) Procedural Timeline Violations & Denial of 
Meaningful Notice 
This dispute was not denied on the merits. 
It was denied through a procedural structure that deprived the consumer of a meaningful 
opportunity to respond. 
Timeline of Events 
(As communicated by First Interstate Bank on a recorded line): 
 • Dispute formally filed: August 13, 2025 
 • Bank letter dated August 21, 2025 allegedly sent (never received) 
 • Documentation requested September 11, 2025 
 • Full documentation packet already submitted September 5, 2025 
 • Temporary credit issued September 25, 2025 
 • Merchant rebuttal dated November 13, 2025 
 • Bank mailed notice November 14, 2025 
 • Response deadline November 24, 2025 (~9 days) 
 • Case closed for alleged non-response 
2.0) Response Window Defect 
The response window provided to the consumer was: 
- less than two weeks to respond 
- physical mail as the sole delivery method 
- sent while the bank had confirmed knowledge the consumer was traveling 
- refusal to provide digital delivery despite repeated requests 
- inconsistent delivery practices (personal account documents delivered digitally, dispute 
documents withheld) 
- refusal to send documentation to the verified residential address upon request 
- no supplemental notice via call, email, or secure message
The consumer never received the letter. 
No meaningful notice occurred. 
Even as of January 30, 2026, the physical correspondence has not been received. 
14 

A dispute cannot be closed for non-response when the notice structure prevents the consumer 
from receiving the notice. 
That is not procedural compliance. 
That is constructive denial of notice. 
The consumer formally requested an additional copy of the bank’s response and the merchant’s 
rebuttal documentation be mailed to the verified residential address on file. The address was 
confirmed with a bank agent on a recorded call. 
As of January 30, 2026, the requested documentation has not been received. 
2.1) Mastercard Processing Irregularity 
According to Mastercard dispute processing standards, merchant rebuttals must occur within 
structured network timelines designed to ensure fair dispute resolution. 
The merchant rebuttal in this case occurred approximately 92 days after the dispute was filed. 
The consumer was given 9 days to respond. 
This creates an extreme imbalance in procedural opportunity. 
Network dispute systems are designed to ensure: 
 • timely merchant participation 
 • reasonable consumer response opportunity 
 • equitable procedural structure 
 • good-faith processing 
Where one party is granted extended response time while the other is denied meaningful notice, 
the integrity of the dispute process is compromised. 
This is not consistent with reasonable network dispute handling. 
2.2) Creditor Knowledge of Travel Status 
The bank had actual knowledge that the consumer was traveling for work. 
This is documented by repeated calls regarding: 
- card travel authorization 
- location confirmations 
- fraud decline reversals 
- account access discussions 
15 
13300438.1.9-22

Despite this knowledge, the bank continued to rely exclusively on physical mail, despite 
repeated requests from consumer for digital delivery. (The same as how his personal account 
documents are delivered digitally with First Interstate Bank) 
This creates constructive denial of notice. 
2.3) Legal Characterization 
Closing a billing dispute based on a response window the consumer never had a practical ability 
to meet constitutes: 
 • denial of meaningful notice 
 • procedural unfairness 
 • failure of reasonable investigation 
 • creditor bad-faith handling 
 • constructive deprivation of dispute rights 
This conduct is inconsistent with: 
Regulation Z - 12 CFR §1026.13 
 requiring reasonable investigation of billing disputes 
Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards 
 prohibiting unfair or deceptive creditor practices 
Good-faith creditor obligations 
 under federal consumer credit law 
The dispute was closed through procedure - not evidence. 
2.4) Procedural Impact 
A creditor cannot close a billing dispute based on non-response when the notice method makes 
response impossible. 
That is not procedural compliance. 
That is procedural failure. 
Under federal consumer credit standards, investigations must be: 
- Reasonable 
- good-faith 
- accessible 
- not structured to guarantee consumer default 
16 

Closing a dispute on a letter the consumer never received is inconsistent with: 
- Regulation Z dispute handling duties 
- reasonable investigation standards 
- Dodd-Frank UDAAP unfairness principles 
- creditor good-faith obligations 
2.5) Merchant Timeline Irregularity 
The bank states the merchant was given 60 days to respond. 
Standard Mastercard dispute procedures do not grant merchants unlimited response time, 
particularly where services were not provided. 
The consumer was given 9 days. 
The merchant was given 60. 
This imbalance is procedurally unreasonable and materially prejudicial. 
2.6) Mastercard Compliance Obligations 
Under Mastercard dispute processing rules, the issuing bank is required to: 
 • evaluate chargeback eligibility based on delivery of services 
 • not elevate unsigned merchant terms above transaction evidence 
 • treat non-delivery as a valid reason code event 
 • prevent merchants from curing non-performance with token activity 
Where the merchant fails to deliver the contracted service, Mastercard standards require the 
dispute to proceed under: Reason Code 4853 - Goods or Services Not Provided 
Failure to properly process an eligible dispute is a network compliance issue, not a discretionary 
judgment call. 
2.7) Material Risk to the Creditor 
When a bank denies a dispute supported by documentary evidence of non-delivery, the issue is 
no longer a consumer complaint - it becomes a regulatory concern. 
Regulators evaluate patterns of: 
17 
13300438.1.10-22

 • improper dispute denials 
 • superficial investigations 
 • reliance on merchant narratives over evidence 
 • procedural closures without substantive review 
These patterns trigger supervisory review because they undermine the consumer protections 
embedded in federal credit law. 
This case presents a documented record where: 
- evidence exists 
- delivery does not exist 
- denial persists anyway 
- 
That is the definition of investigative failure. 
SECTION 3 - Quantified Regulatory Exposure & Statutory
Liability 
3.1) Truth in Lending Act / Regulation Z Civil Liability (15 
U.S.C. §1640; 12 CFR §1026.13) 
Legal anchor: Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) imposes billing error investigation and correction 
duties. The private right of action and civil liability framework is under the Truth in Lending Act 
(15 U.S.C. §1640). 
Recoverable categories in an individual consumer action may include: 
● Actual damages (fact-based harm caused by mishandling) 
● Statutory damages (subject to statutory limits in 15 U.S.C. §1640) 
● Court costs 
● Reasonable attorney’s fees (fee shifting upon prevailing) 
3.1.A) Repeated Dispute Mishandling Increases Legal Exposure (Without 
Overstating Caps) 
This matter involves multiple discrete procedural failures, including: 
18 

● improper denial despite documented non-delivery, 
● repeated “missing documentation” assertions despite submission, 
● procedural closure tied to notice that was never received, 
● appeal-level repetition of the same investigative defects. 
While statutory damages in an individual action are capped under §1640, each discrete failure 
can still support independent liability theories and increases: 
● actual damages exposure, 
● attorney-fee exposure, and 
● the reasonableness of regulatory scrutiny when conduct continues after notice. 
3.1.B) Conservative Civil Exposure Model (Defensible) 
A conservative litigation exposure model typically includes: 
● Statutory damages (capped by statute) 
● Attorney’s fees (fee shifting): commonly $25,000-$75,000+ in contested 
consumer-credit litigation (fact-dependent) 
● Costs: $2,000-$10,000 (filing, service, transcripts) 
Even conservatively, the fee-shifting reality alone can make the exposure materially exceed the 
underlying principal dispute. 
3.2) Restitution / Account Remediation Exposure 
If the dispute is corrected on the merits, the creditor must unwind the transaction effects: 
● Disputed principal: $10,500 
● Accrued interest (variable) 
● Any penalty or late fees triggered by the balance 
● Credit reporting correction (if impacted) 
This represents the clean remediation path. 
3.3) Dodd-Frank UDAAP Civil Penalty Framework 
(Regulatory context, not a consumer demand) 
19 
13300438.1.11-22

Under Dodd-Frank, regulators may impose civil money penalties for unfair or deceptive 
practices. Current inflation-adjusted tiers: 
- Tier 1: $7,217 per day 
- Tier 2 (reckless): $36,083 per day 
- Tier 3 (knowing): $1,443,275 per day 
Example 10-day enforcement proxy: 
Tier 1 → $72,170 
 Tier 2 → $360,830 
 Tier 3 → $14,432,750 
This illustrates supervisory exposure environment, not a private claim. 
3.3.A) Escalation Risk When Conduct Becomes Reckless 
UDAAP tiers escalate based on mental state. 
Once a creditor continues improper handling after notice, regulators evaluate conduct under 
reckless or knowing tiers. 
Patterns that trigger Tier 2 review include: 
● repeated denial after notice 
● ignoring documentary evidence 
● procedural closure without investigation 
Example 5-day Tier 2 window: 
$36,083 × 5 = $180,415 
This demonstrates why dispute handling is compliance-critical. 
3.4) Merchant-Side Deceptive Practices Exposure 
(Context) 
If the merchant’s inducement claims and post-payment “term” assertions are proven deceptive, 
the merchant may face liability under applicable state consumer protection and common-law 
fraud principles. 
This is included as context because it reinforces a core dispute fact: a merchant cannot retain 
payment for an undelivered service by retroactively asserting unsigned terms, and the 
20 

bank should not treat unilateral, post-payment assertions as binding contractual terms where 
there is no mutual assent.

Even a modest consumer credit lawsuit commonly produces: 
- Attorney fees: $25,000 - $100,000+ 
- Discovery / evidence handling: $5,000 - $25,000 
- Depositions / expert review: $5,000 - $20,000 
Total practical litigation environment: $35,000 - $145,000+ independent of the $10,500 principal. 
3.6) Consolidated Conservative Exposure Summary 
TILA/Reg Z statutory + fees: $47,000 - $105,000+ 
Restitution/remediation: $10,500 + interest/fees 
Litigation cost environment: $35,000 - $145,000+ 
Regulatory penalty exposure: supervisory context only

and escalates dramatically under supervisory review.

Federal law expressly prohibits retaliation or adverse action against a consumer for asserting 
protected rights.

Regulation B: 
● A creditor may not discriminate against or penalize a consumer 
 for exercising rights under consumer credit protection laws. 
● Adverse treatment linked to dispute activity, complaints, or enforcement efforts is 
prohibited.

● This dispute and its resolution will not be reported or characterized negatively 
● No adverse action, account limitation, repricing, or relationship impairment

This correspondence concerns confirmed violations of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. 
§1666), Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards, arising from First

Based on the documented record already in First Interstate Bank’s possession, liability exposure 
is established as a matter of law. These violations give rise to statutory damages, actual 
damages, mandatory attorney’s fees, restitution, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties, 
independent of any merchant conduct. 
Jurisdiction for enforcement and adjudication is preserved in parallel before federal regulators, 
state banking authorities, and state and federal courts. This letter is not sent in anticipation of 
dispute, but to afford the issuer a final administrative opportunity to correct the billing error and 
mitigate escalating regulatory and litigation exposure. 
Administrative resolution at this stage is the most efficient and least disruptive outcome. 
13460363.1.2-12

I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure (Read First)

I. Statutory Basis & Issuer Exposure 
(Read First) 
This matter arises from First Interstate Bank’s mishandling of a federally protected billing 
error dispute, in violation of the Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666), Regulation Z (12 
C.F.R. §1026.13), and federal UDAAP standards enforced by the Consumer Financial 
Protection Bureau. 
I.A. Trigger Event - Notice of Billing Error 
• Once First Interstate Bank received notice of a billing error, the bank assumed independent, 
non-delegable statutory duties. 
I.B. Duty 1 - Reasonable Investigation 
 • Conduct a reasonable investigation (§1666(a); Reg Z §1026.13(c)) 
I.C. Duty 2 - No Collection / Adverse Action During Dispute 
 • Refrain from collection or adverse action during the dispute (§1666(a)(3)(B)(ii)) 
I.D. Duty 3 - Written Explanation + Supporting Documentation 
 • Provide a written explanation of any denial addressing the consumer’s evidence 
(§1666(a)(3)(B); Reg Z §1026.13(f)) and provide supporting documentation upon request. 
I.E. Duty 4 - No Reliance on Merchant Assertions / Unenforceable Policies 
 • Avoid reliance on merchant assertions or unenforceable policies 

Failure to meet these duties exposes the issuer to statutory damages, actual damages, 
attorney’s fees, regulatory enforcement, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 
These obligations apply regardless of merchant refund policies, alleged service hours, or 
internal merchant narratives. 
I.0 Summary of Issuer Violations & Unavoidable 
Liability Exposure 
Based on the documented record, First Interstate Bank has already incurred multiple, 
independent violations of federal billing-error and consumer-protection law, each of which 
carries separate and cumulative liability. 
These violations include, but are not limited to: 
A. Failure to Conduct a Reasonable Investigation 
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(c)) 
 - sustaining a denial without proof of agreed terms or actual delivery. 
B. Improper Reliance on Merchant Assertions 
 (Reg Z; CFPB UDAAP standards) 
 - accepting an undisclosed, unsigned, retroactive billing theory in direct contradiction of the 
documented transaction. 
C. Defective Denial and Withholding of Supporting Documentation 
 (15 U.S.C. §1666(a)(3)(B); 12 C.F.R. §1026.13(f)) 
 - failing to provide a denial that meaningfully addresses the consumer’s evidence and refusing 
access to the representment record. 
D. Procedural Unfairness and Burden Shifting 
 (CFPB UDAAP; FTC Act §5 principles) 
 - depriving the consumer of a meaningful opportunity to respond while granting the merchant 
extended deadlines. 
Each of the above violations independently triggers civil liability under the Truth in Lending 
Act (15 U.S.C. §1640), including: 
• Statutory damages 
 • Actual damages 
 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs 
Once notice was provided and enforcement continued, exposure escalated from negligent to 
knowing or reckless noncompliance, materially increasing regulatory, supervisory, and 
litigation risk. 
13460363.1.3-12

If litigated, liability does not depend on disputed facts or credibility determinations. 
 It is established by the bank’s own investigation posture, documented record, and continued 
reliance on unenforceable merchant assertions after notice. 
Administrative correction at this stage avoids locking in a provable issuer-side compliance 
failure that would otherwise survive regulatory, audit, and judicial review. 
I.0.1 Liability Is Inevitable if Litigation or Regulatory 
Review Occurs 
If this matter proceeds to litigation or regulatory adjudication, issuer liability is unavoidable 
based on the existing record. 
This outcome does not depend on disputed facts, witness credibility, or competing narratives. It 
flows directly from: 
• The bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation after notice 
 • Reliance on an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed merchant billing theory 
 • Enforcement of non-existent contractual terms 
 • Procedural deprivation of the consumer’s statutory response rights 
 • Continued denial after actual notice of non-delivery and lack of assent 
Once notice was provided, continued enforcement converted the issue from a merchant 
dispute into an issuer-side compliance violation under the FCBA, Regulation Z, and 
CFPB-enforced UDAAP standards. 
Courts and regulators do not permit issuers to avoid liability by deferring to merchant assertions 
where: 
 (a) no enforceable agreement exists, and 
 (b) promised outcomes were never delivered. 
Accordingly, liability attaches as a matter of law, not discretion. 
Administrative correction at this stage is not a concession - it is the only action consistent with 
statutory compliance, risk containment, and supervisory expectations. 
II. PLAIN-ENGLISH EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 

(this frames everything that follows) 
This dispute is not just “services not rendered.” 
It is a pattern of misrepresentation, lack of agreement, retroactive billing, and procedural 
unfairness, compounded by the bank’s failure to conduct a reasonable investigation. There is a 
pattern of conduct that, once notice was provided, supports findings of willful non-compliance, 
willful negligence, willful misconduct, and reckless disregard. 
II.1 What Was Purchased & Promised 
● I purchased a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb service, NOT consulting. 
● The merchant promised (verified in already provided documentation: ads, website, 
sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) to: 
○ Acquire a property 
○ Furnish it 
○ List it 
○ Deliver a cash-flowing Airbnb generating ~$3,500-$5,000+/month 
○ With explicit representations such as: “If we don’t acquire you a property, you 
pay $0.” 
○ Explicit week by week timeline of deliverables 
● None of that happened. (all proven and verified factually and indisputably via already 
provided ads, website, sales calls, written materials, graphics, and transcripts) 
○ No property acquired 
○ No lease signed 
○ No furnishing 
○ No listing 
○ No revenue 
II.2 Post-Failure Recharacterization - Non-Delivery 
Downscale, Partial Refund, and Unenforceable 
Retroactive Billing (109 Hours / $45 per Hour) 
13460363.1.4-12

Documented & Provided Record confirms non-delivery and downscale/partial refund: The 
merchant acknowledged missed timelines/non-delivery and agreed to a downscale (from two 
properties to one) and issued a partial refund of $7,500 (as documented in the provided 
texts/emails). 
After continued non-delivery, a billing error dispute was initiated: After the merchant still 
failed to deliver the promised DFY (Done-For-You) outcome, the consumer initiated 
billing-error/chargeback remedies based on misrepresentation, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
(Documentation already provided multiple times) 
Following the partial refund and downscale, the consumer granted the merchant an 
additional extension and opportunity to deliver the remaining single promised property.
Despite this extension and additional time beyond the original and revised timelines, the 
merchant failed to acquire, furnish, list, or deliver even one revenue-producing Airbnb 
property as promised, marketed, advertised, and transcribed. This extended non-delivery is 
documented in the record. 
In response to the chargeback claim, following their second opportunity for failed delivery of 
promised and fraudulently induced misrepresented deliverables of a DFY Airbnb profiting , 
furnished, & listed property, the merchant responds that: 
● They retroactively claimed the money was for “consulting” 
● They alleged 109 hours at $45/hour to justify retaining $10,500 (unsupported merchant 
allegation; unproven, undocumented, and never consented to) - notwithstanding that the 
consumer’s participation consisted of occasional, optionally scheduled weekly calls of 
approximately one hour.
○ The asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” figure is not a term agreed to by the 
consumer, nor is it supported by any executed agreement, disclosure, or 
consent. This figure is an unsupported allegation made by the merchant 
and relayed by the bank, introduced only after the merchant failed to 
deliver the promised Done-For-You Airbnb service. At no point did the 
consumer agree to hourly consulting, time-based billing, or any pricing 
structure contingent on hours worked rather than outcome delivered. The 
consumer’s actual participation consisted of occasional weekly update 
calls of approximately one hour, which were ancillary to the promised DFY 
service and never represented or disclosed as billable consulting. 
No contemporaneous time records, invoices, or consumer-acknowledged logs have been 
produced. 
● This hourly billing: 
○ Was never disclosed 
○ Was never agreed to 
○ Was never signed 

○ Was never part of the deal 
● In reality, I only had occasional weekly calls (~1 hour each), which were calls to keep 
me up to date, not paid hourly consulting. 
II.3 Net Impression + Issuer UDAAP Exposure 
Under FTC Act §5, the transaction is defined by the net impression of the merchant’s 
advertising and sales representations. 
All representations in this matter concerned a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and handover, 
not hourly consulting. 
The merchant’s post-hoc claim of ‘109 hours at $45/hour’ was never disclosed, never agreed to, 
and is materially inconsistent with the advertised deliverables, including express representations 
that no payment would be owed absent property acquisition. (unsupported merchant allegation; 
no signed agreement, disclosure, or consumer consent exists) 
The FTC treats such retroactive recharacterization as deceptive, unreasonable, and 
unenforceable. Reliance on unsigned or undisclosed terms cannot cure or override deceptive 
advertising. 
These FTC Act §5 standards are directly applicable to First Interstate Bank as the issuer 
responsible for conducting a reasonable investigation and for avoiding unfair or 
deceptive practices in dispute resolution. 
Once the bank is placed on notice that a merchant’s post-transaction narrative is materially 
inconsistent with the net impression of the advertising and sales representations, the bank may 
not lawfully rely on that narrative to deny a billing error claim. 
II.3A) Compliance 
Accepting and enforcing an undisclosed, unsigned, and retroactively imposed billing 
theory, particularly one that contradicts express outcome-based promises; exposes the bank 
to liability for facilitating or perpetuating an unfair practice, failing to conduct a 
reasonable investigation, and improperly shifting the burden of proof to the consumer. 
Federal regulators, including the CFPB, evaluate such conduct as an independent compliance 
failure, regardless of merchant misconduct, where the issuer disregards clear evidence of 
deceptive inducement, lack of assent, and non-delivery. 
13460363.1.5-12

Continued enforcement after notice materially increases regulatory exposure, corrective action 
risk, restitution obligations, and supervisory findings. 
II.3B) Notice 
Once First Interstate Bank was placed on actual notice of the material misrepresentations, lack 
of mutual assent, unenforceable and unsigned terms, and total non-delivery of the promised 
Done-For-You Airbnb service, the bank assumed an independent legal obligation to correct the 
billing error and conduct a reasonable, evidence-based investigation. At that point, continued 
reliance on the merchant’s post-hoc narrative, particularly one that contradicts the net 
impression of the merchant’s own advertising and sales representations - constitutes a separate 
and independent compliance failure by the issuer. 
Issuer obligations under the FCBA and Regulation Z are non-delegable and cannot be satisfied 
by deferring to merchant assertions where the evidentiary record contradicts those assertions. 
Federal regulators do not permit issuers to defer to merchant assertions where the 
record demonstrates deceptive inducement, non-delivery, and lack of consumer consent. 
Continued enforcement after notice exposes the bank to issuer-side liability under 
Regulation Z, UDAAP standards enforced by the CFPB, and unfair-practice facilitation 
principles recognized under the FTC Act, regardless of whether the merchant itself is 
separately pursued. 
Issuer must prove (or obtain from the merchant) evidence of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual 
delivery; absent that, denial is noncompliant and cannot rest on post-hoc assertions. 
II.4 No Contract / No Assent / No Enforceable Terms 
There is no enforceable agreement supporting the merchant’s claims: 
● No signed contract 
● No DocuSign 
● No click-wrap 
● No assent 
● No meeting of the minds 
● No agreement authorizing hourly billing 
● No agreement allowing changed terms 
● No agreement making the transaction non-refundable 
Because no executed agreement exists authorizing hourly consulting, retroactive billing, 
modified deliverables, or non-refundability, the merchant’s asserted “109 hours at $45/hour” 

theory is a legal nullity. Under basic contract law principles, a party cannot impose material 
pricing terms, scope changes, or billing structures absent mutual assent. An unsigned, 
undisclosed, or unaccepted document - whether characterized as “terms,” “policies,” or “internal 
agreements” - has no legal force and cannot be enforced by the merchant or adopted by the 
issuer. Any attempt to retain funds on this basis constitutes retention without contractual or legal 
authority. 
II.4A Required Proof to Sustain Denial (Must Be 
Produced) 
Required proof to sustain denial (must be produced): (a) signed governing agreement; 
(b) signed hourly billing authorization; (c) itemized time records acknowledged by 
consumer; (d) proof of disclosure pre-payment; (e) proof of delivery of DFY outcomes; (f) 
full representment packet and case log.
Despite this, the bank: 
● Enforced unsigned terms 
● Shifted the burden of proof to the consumer 
● Accepted merchant allegations without proof 
● Denied access to rebuttal documents 
● Gave the merchant months to respond 
● Gave the consumer less than 10 days to respond - via a letter never received 
II.5 Denial Basis = Procedure, Not Delivery 
The denial was not based on evidence of delivery.
It was based on procedural technicalities and an unenforceable merchant narrative. 
Maintaining denial under these circumstances, where the issuer has been provided 
overwhelming, documented evidence of deceptive inducement, non-delivery, lack of assent, and 
unenforceable terms - creates escalating regulatory exposure for First Interstate Bank. 
Under CFPB enforcement authority, issuers that engage in or perpetuate unfair, deceptive, or 
unreasonable dispute-resolution practices are subject to restitution orders, corrective action 
mandates, supervisory findings, and civil money penalties. 
13460363.1.6-12

CFPB civil penalty tiers currently authorize fines of up to $5,000 per day for standard violations, 
up to $25,000 per day for reckless violations, and up to $1,000,000 per day for knowing 
violations, with exposure increasing once an institution has actual notice and continues the 
challenged conduct. 
Administrative correction at this stage is the only resolution consistent with regulatory 
expectations and risk management principles. 
Comparable issuer-side correction after escalation has been granted in prior CFPB-supervised 
billing error matters where provisional credit was ultimately made permanent following issuer 
escalation. 
III. Issuer Liability, Penalties, and 
Litigation Exposure 
If First Interstate Bank continues to enforce an unenforceable merchant narrative after notice, 
the bank faces escalating exposure under multiple independent authorities, including but not 
limited to: 
1. Fair Credit Billing Act (15 U.S.C. §1666) 
 • Statutory damages as authorized under the Truth in Lending Act and Fair Credit Billing Act 
civil liability provisions (15 U.S.C. §1640), together with: 
 • Actual damages (interest, fees, credit harm, consequential loss) 
 • Mandatory attorney’s fees and costs 
2. Regulation Z (12 C.F.R. §1026.13) 
 • Civil liability for failure to investigate 
 • Liability for defective notice and explanation 
 • Liability for unlawful collection activity during dispute 
3. CFPB UDAAP Authority (12 U.S.C. §§5531–5536) 
 Civil money penalties of: 
 - Up to $5,000 per day (standard violations) 
 - Up to $25,000 per day (reckless violations) 
 - Up to $1,000,000 per day (knowing violations) 
 - Restitution orders 
 - Corrective action mandates 
 - Supervisory findings and compliance downgrades 

4. FTC Act §5 (15 U.S.C. §45) 
 • Liability for facilitating or perpetuating unfair or deceptive practices once on notice 
 • Issuer responsibility where enforcement enables merchant deception 
Based on the documented timeline, repeated notice, and continued enforcement, First Interstate 
Bank’s exposure substantially exceeds the amount in dispute. Correction at this stage materially 
reduces regulatory, litigation, and reputational risk. 
This is why this matter involves: 
● Fraudulent inducement 
● Material misrepresentation 
● Lack of mutual assent 
● Retroactive billing 
● Unfair and unreasonable investigation practices 
● Willful or reckless disregard once notice was provided 
● Willful non-compliance 
● Willful misconduct 
The checklist below breaks this down line-by-line, provable fact-by-fact. 
III.A Operational, Network, and Supervisory Exposure 
(Primary Executive Risk) 
Independent of statutory penalties, continued enforcement of this denial creates 
immediate issuer-side operational and supervisory exposure that materially 
exceeds the amount in dispute. 
Card-network dispute rules (Visa / Mastercard) require issuers to base denials 
on proof of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery. Where a merchant relies on 
undisclosed, unsigned, or retroactively imposed terms - and where delivery of the 
promised service did not occur - issuers may not lawfully sustain a denial or shift 
the burden to the cardholder. 
By upholding this denial after documented notice of non-delivery, lack of 
assent, and unenforceable terms, the bank assumes direct issuer 
responsibility for: 
• A defective investigation finding 
 • Improper reliance on merchant assertions 
 • Network-rule noncompliance 
 • Loss allocation shifting from merchant to issuer 
 • Audit and supervisory exception risk 
13460363.1.7-12

Once a billing error dispute reaches this posture, continued enforcement 
converts a merchant dispute into an issuer-side compliance failure. At that 
point, loss exposure is no longer capped by the transaction amount and becomes: 
• An internal loss event 
 • A compliance exception subject to supervisory review 
 • A precedent case impacting future disputes 
 • A documented failure of Reg Z investigation standards
Issuer-side correction at this stage is the standard risk-mitigation response used 
to avoid locking in a provable investigation defect that survives escalation, audit, or 
litigation review. 
This is why similarly situated issuers routinely reverse and make provisional 
credit permanent after escalation - not because of merchant fault, but 
because issuer-side exposure materially exceeds the disputed amount. 
IV - MASTER LEGAL & COMPLIANCE 
CHECKLIST 
(Black-and-white, verifiable, no speculation)
The issuer must base its decision on evidence of (i) agreed terms and (ii) actual delivery - 
not post-hoc merchant narratives. 
For avoidance of doubt, the burden of proof in this matter rests with the merchant and 
the issuer - not the consumer. Where a merchant asserts alternative deliverables, 
modified pricing, or non-refundability, the merchant and issuer must prove the existence 
of a valid, enforceable agreement reflecting those terms. The consumer is not required to 
disprove unsupported allegations or reconstruct a transaction that was never agreed to. 
The record already contains extensive affirmative proof establishing the original DFY 
scope, the promised outcomes, and the absence of delivery. 
Absent production of a signed agreement expressly authorizing alternative pricing or 
deliverables, no further rebuttal is legally required from the consumer.

Plain Statement of the Issue
This matter is straightforward.
I paid a merchant for a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment service that was
clearly promised, clearly defined, and never delivered.
The merchant accepted payment, failed to deliver the promised services, missed all agreed
timelines, and later attempted to justify non-delivery by asserting “terms” and billing concepts
that were never presented, never agreed to, and never signed by me.
Those post-payment terms are unenforceable as a matter of law and cannot be used to
defeat a services-not-rendered claim.
Despite this, First Interstate Bank has treated the merchant’s unilateral, unsigned assertions as
if they were governing contractual terms - even though no valid agreement exists reflecting
those provisions.
I have attached extensive, undeniable documentation originating from the merchant itself -
including advertising, invoices, sales representations, written communications, and recorded
statements - all of which establish:
● what was promised
● that payment was made
● that delivery never occurred
This is not a quality dispute or a misunderstanding.
This is a documented services-not-rendered dispute involving misrepresentation and fraudulent
inducement.
Under consumer protection law and Mastercard dispute standards, a merchant cannot cure
non-delivery by inventing or retroactively enforcing terms that were never agreed to, and a bank
cannot rely on such terms to deny a valid dispute.
I am requesting that First Interstate Bank correct this matter immediately by refunding the
remaining $10,500, plus all associated interest and fees, consistent with federal consumer credit
law and network dispute rules.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This submission concerns a billing dispute arising from a paid transaction for a Done-For-You
Airbnb acquisition and deployment program that was never delivered.
This dispute is not ambiguous. It is not a quality complaint. It is not a misunderstanding. It is a
documented services-not-rendered event supported by evidence of:
● misrepresentation
● failure of consideration
● total non-performance
The merchant accepted payment for a fixed-fee program promising:
● property acquisition
● furnishing and deployment
● professional listing launch
● immediate profitability
After more than eleven weeks:
● no property was acquired
● no deployment occurred
● no revenue-producing system existed
The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery through unsigned post-payment terms and
token activity unrelated to the essential purpose of the contract. That conduct cannot legally
cure non-performance.
The evidentiary record is binary:
● promised service exists
● payment exists
● delivery does not exist
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), this satisfies
chargeback eligibility.
Despite this, the issuing bank denied the dispute and subsequent appeals under a false premise
that documentation was missing, even though the complete documentation packet had already
been submitted and confirmed.
5

The dispute was ultimately closed based on an alleged non-response tied to a physical letter the
consumer never received, during a period when:
● the bank had confirmed knowledge the consumer was traveling
● digital delivery of dispute communications was repeatedly requested
● physical-mail-only notice was used anyway
The dispute was therefore closed through procedure rather than evidence.
1) Services Not Rendered
1.1) Verified Promises vs. Verified Non-Delivery
This dispute is a services-not-rendered matter supported by
- documented misrepresentation
- failure of consideration
- total non-delivery of the merchant’s core promised deliverables
The merchant marketed and sold a Done-For-You (DFY) Airbnb acquisition and deployment
system operation that promised:
- rapid property acquisition
- full furnishing and setup
- professional listing launch
- and immediate profitability.
These promises are not interpretation
They are documented in the merchant’s own:
- public advertising
- onboarding materials
- Invoice
- written marketing
- recorded sales statements
6

1.2) Merchant’s Promises - Verifiable Evidence
The merchant represented that the consumer would receive:
• acquisition of a landlord-approved Airbnb property
• full interior design and furnishing
• professional photography
• automated listing launch and optimization
• bookings and revenue generation
• target profit of $3,500-$5,000+ per month per property beginning month 1
• ability to launch a $10K+ per month Airbnb first try
• rapid execution timeline (weeks, not months)
These representations are verified by:
• merchant website screenshots advertising $10K+/month Airbnb launches
• merchant promotional materials stating $3.5K+ monthly average profit
• merchant visual timeline graphic promising rapid 90-day deployment
• merchant branded PDF describing DFY acquisition + setup
• recorded sales call transcript confirming week-by-week execution plan & profits
• written flyer stating: “You pay $0 if we do not find you a property.”
• the issued invoice documenting a fixed-fee DFY acquisition program
Each of these items is attached and labeled for independent verification.
They originate from the merchant, not the consumer.
This establishes:
- clear inducement terms
- performance expectations
- reliance
1.3) Verified Outcome After Eleven Weeks
After more than eleven weeks from payment:
• no property was acquired
• no lease was secured
• no furnishing occurred
• no design work was completed
• no photography was scheduled
• no listing went live
• no bookings occurred
7

• no revenue was generated
• no DFY system was deployed
• no promised deliverable was fulfilled
This is not partial performance.
This is total non-delivery of the core service purchased.
1.4) Merchant’s Post-Payment Defense
The merchant later attempted to justify non-delivery by:
- introducing unsigned, post-payment “terms”
- asserting retroactive hourly billing
- presenting rules never disclosed
- presenting documents never agreed to
- presenting documents never signed
The merchant also attempts to characterize delivery as:
temporary access to shared AIRDNA software costing approximately
$34-$125/month
This software access:
• expired after 3-4 weeks
• is a low-cost third-party tool
• provides inaccurate data
• has trivial commercial value
• does not replace the contracted service
This is not performance.
This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat a chargeback.
1.4.A) Preparatory Activity Cannot Satisfy the Essential
Purpose of the Contract
The merchant may argue that access to analytics software or preliminary research constituted
partial performance.
Even assuming analytics research is a preparatory component of the workflow, preparatory
steps do not equal delivery of the contracted service
.
The transaction was not for consulting, research assistance, or software access.
8

The transaction was for a Done-For-You Airbnb acquisition and deployment resulting in an
operational, furnished, revenue-generating property.
Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential purpose of the
agreement - not by isolated preparatory activity.
See:
• Restatement (Second) of Contracts §235
• UCC failure of consideration principles
• Texas contract law on essential purpose and material breach
The essential purpose required:
• acquisition of a landlord-approved property
• furnishing and interior deployment
• listing activation
• operational launch
• revenue generation capability
None of these occurred.
Where the essential purpose of a contract fails, preparatory work cannot convert
non-performance into performance.
Courts consistently reject the argument that incomplete preparation satisfies a contract whose
central objective was never achieved.
At most, analytics access represents abandoned preliminary activity that never matured into
delivery.
This is legally classified as:
• failure of consideration
• material breach
• services not rendered
• unjust enrichment if payment is retained
A party cannot retain full payment by pointing to incomplete preparation when the contracted
result was never delivered.
That principle applies equally in contract law and card network dispute frameworks.
1.4.B) Token Activity Cannot Cure Total Non-Delivery
9

Providing temporary access to low-cost third-party software does not constitute delivery of a
high-value DFY deployment system.
This is not partial fulfillment.
This is an attempt to manufacture the appearance of activity to defeat chargeback eligibility.
Mastercard dispute standards evaluate:
- delivery of the contracted service
- not peripheral activity
- not unrelated tools
- not token gestures
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853 (Goods or Services Not Provided), the test is binary:
Was the promised service delivered as agreed?
Here:
- no property was acquired
- no listing was launched
- no system was operational
- no revenue capability existed
Therefore:
the contracted service was not delivered.
Token activity cannot substitute for contractual performance.
Allowing merchants to defeat disputes by pointing to trivial or unrelated activity would nullify
consumer protection frameworks entirely.
Card network rules explicitly prevent this outcome.
1.4.C) Retention of Payment After Non-Performance
Creates Unjust Enrichment
If a merchant retains full payment after failing to deliver the essential purpose of the contract,
the legal consequence is unjust enrichment.
Unjust enrichment occurs when:
- payment is received
- the bargained-for benefit is not delivered
- the recipient retains the money anyway
Courts treat unjust enrichment as an equitable remedy independent of contract disputes.
10

Even absent formal contract analysis, equity requires restitution when performance fails.
This principle reinforces chargeback eligibility.
The consumer paid for a result.
The result does not exist.
Retention of funds under those circumstances is legally indefensible.
1.5) Legal Characterization of Merchant Conduct
Post-payment contract fabrication cannot cure non-performance.
Token software access cannot substitute for a DFY business deployment.
This conduct independently constitutes deceptive practice under:
• FTC Act §5 - unfair or deceptive acts
• Texas Deceptive Trade Practices Act §17.46
• failure of consideration doctrine
• common law fraudulent inducement principles
The essential purpose of the contract was never performed.
Under contract law:
trivial acts do not satisfy primary obligations
1.6) Legal Classification of the Dispute
This dispute is a textbook case of:
• Goods/Services Not Provided
• Failure of Consideration
• Misrepresentation
• Fraudulent Inducement
• Unenforceable Post-Payment Contract Attempt
Under Mastercard Reason Code 4853
(Goods or Services Not Provided)
chargeback eligibility is established when promised services are not delivered.
The merchant’s attempt to rewrite the agreement after payment does not eliminate original
non-delivery.
11

1.6.A) KEY LEGAL POINT: Unenforceable Post-Payment
Terms
A contract requires mutual assent.
The merchant has produced no signed agreement, no acceptance record, and no
contemporaneous disclosure showing that the consumer agreed to the “terms” now
being asserted.
Terms introduced after payment - or never disclosed at all - are legally
unenforceable and cannot be used to:
● justify non-delivery
● convert non-performance into performance
● defeat consumer protection rights
Treating unilateral, unsigned merchant assertions as binding terms contradicts
basic contract law principles and cannot support a valid dispute denial.
Additionally, even if a merchant claims “terms exist,” the operative question is
whether there is evidence of mutual assent at or before payment (signature,
clickwrap acceptance record, or contemporaneous disclosure tied to the
transaction).
The merchant has provided no enforceable acceptance record for the post-payment
terms now being asserted. Therefore, those terms cannot defeat a
services-not-rendered claim or convert non-performance into performance.
1.7) Binary Record of the Case
The record is legally binary when evaluated against the contracted service.
● A promised Done-For-You Airbnb deployment service exists
● Payment for that service exists
● The contracted service was never performed
- No property was acquired.
12

- No Airbnb was launched.
- No DFY system was deployed.
Any unrelated third-party software access referenced by the merchant does not
constitute delivery of the contracted service and has no bearing on performance
analysis.
Under contract law, performance is measured by fulfillment of the essential
purpose of the agreement. That essential purpose was never achieved.
This satisfies the legal definition of: Services Not Rendered.
1.8) Creditor Investigation Duties & Bank Liability
Once a services-not-rendered dispute is supported by documentary evidence, the issuing bank
is not permitted to rely solely on merchant rebuttal narratives that contradict the factual record.
Under Regulation Z - 12 CFR §1026.13, the creditor must:
• conduct a reasonable investigation
• review submitted documentation in good faith
• resolve disputes based on evidence
• not shift the burden to the consumer once proof is submitted
• acknowledge disputes within 30 days
• resolve disputes within two billing cycles
Repeated requests for documentation already submitted, followed by denial on a false “missing
documentation” premise, is inconsistent with the creditor’s duty to perform a meaningful
investigation.
A creditor may not close a billing error dispute based on procedural technicalities when the
evidentiary record establishes non-delivery.
Failure to properly investigate exposes the creditor to liability under:
• Truth in Lending Act enforcement provisions
• Regulation Z dispute handling obligations
• Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards
• unfair or deceptive practices by covered entities
• CFPB supervisory expectations for reasonable investigations
13

1.9) Procedural Timeline Violations & Denial of
Meaningful Notice
This dispute was not denied on the merits.
It was denied through a procedural structure that deprived the consumer of a meaningful
opportunity to respond.

• Temporary credit issued September 25, 2025
• Merchant rebuttal dated November 13, 2025
• Bank mailed notice November 14, 2025
• Response deadline November 24, 2025 (~9 days)
• Case closed for alleged non-response
2.0) Response Window Defect
The response window provided to the consumer was:
- less than two weeks to respond
- physical mail as the sole delivery method
- sent while the bank had confirmed knowledge the consumer was traveling
- refusal to provide digital delivery despite repeated requests
- inconsistent delivery practices (personal account documents delivered digitally, dispute
documents withheld)
- refusal to send documentation to the verified residential address upon request
- no supplemental notice via call, email, or secure message
The consumer never received the letter.
No meaningful notice occurred.
Even as of January 30, 2026, the physical correspondence has not been received.
14

A dispute cannot be closed for non-response when the notice structure prevents the consumer
from receiving the notice.
That is not procedural compliance.
That is constructive denial of notice.
The consumer formally requested an additional copy of the bank’s response and the merchant’s
rebuttal documentation be mailed to the verified residential address on file. The address was
confirmed with a bank agent on a recorded call.
As of January 30, 2026, the requested documentation has not been received.
2.1) Mastercard Processing Irregularity
According to Mastercard dispute processing standards, merchant rebuttals must occur within
structured network timelines designed to ensure fair dispute resolution.
The merchant rebuttal in this case occurred approximately 92 days after the dispute was filed.
The consumer was given 9 days to respond.
This creates an extreme imbalance in procedural opportunity.
Network dispute systems are designed to ensure:
• timely merchant participation
• reasonable consumer response opportunity
• equitable procedural structure
• good-faith processing
Where one party is granted extended response time while the other is denied meaningful notice,
the integrity of the dispute process is compromised.
This is not consistent with reasonable network dispute handling.
2.2) Creditor Knowledge of Travel Status
The bank had actual knowledge that the consumer was traveling for work.
This is documented by repeated calls regarding:
- card travel authorization
- location confirmations
- fraud decline reversals
- account access discussions
15

Despite this knowledge, the bank continued to rely exclusively on physical mail, despite
repeated requests from consumer for digital delivery. (The same as how his personal account
documents are delivered digitally with First Interstate Bank)
This creates constructive denial of notice.
2.3) Legal Characterization
Closing a billing dispute based on a response window the consumer never had a practical ability
to meet constitutes:
• denial of meaningful notice
• procedural unfairness
• failure of reasonable investigation
• creditor bad-faith handling
• constructive deprivation of dispute rights
This conduct is inconsistent with:
Regulation Z - 12 CFR §1026.13
requiring reasonable investigation of billing disputes
Dodd-Frank UDAAP standards
prohibiting unfair or deceptive creditor practices
Good-faith creditor obligations
under federal consumer credit law
The dispute was closed through procedure - not evidence.
2.4) Procedural Impact
A creditor cannot close a billing dispute based on non-response when the notice method makes
response impossible.
That is not procedural compliance.
That is procedural failure.
Under federal consumer credit standards, investigations must be:
- Reasonable
- good-faith
- accessible
- not structured to guarantee consumer default
16

Closing a dispute on a letter the consumer never received is inconsistent with:
- Regulation Z dispute handling duties
- reasonable investigation standards
- Dodd-Frank UDAAP unfairness principles
- creditor good-faith obligations
2.5) Merchant Timeline Irregularity
The bank states the merchant was given 60 days to respond.
Standard Mastercard dispute procedures do not grant merchants unlimited response time,
particularly where services were not provided.
The consumer was given 9 days.
The merchant was given 60.
This imbalance is procedurally unreasonable and materially prejudicial.
2.6) Mastercard Compliance Obligations
Under Mastercard dispute processing rules, the issuing bank is required to:
• evaluate chargeback eligibility based on delivery of services
• not elevate unsigned merchant terms above transaction evidence
• treat non-delivery as a valid reason code event
• prevent merchants from curing non-performance with token activity
Where the merchant fails to deliver the contracted service, Mastercard standards require the
dispute to proceed under: Reason Code 4853 - Goods or Services Not Provided
Failure to properly process an eligible dispute is a network compliance issue, not a discretionary
judgment call.
2.7) Material Risk to the Creditor
When a bank denies a dispute supported by documentary evidence of non-delivery, the issue is
no longer a consumer complaint - it becomes a regulatory concern.
Regulators evaluate patterns of:
17

• improper dispute denials
• superficial investigations
• reliance on merchant narratives over evidence
• procedural closures without substantive review
These patterns trigger supervisory review because they undermine the consumer protections
embedded in federal credit law.
This case presents a documented record where:
- evidence exists
- delivery does not exist
- denial persists anyway
-
That is the definition of investigative failure.
SECTION 3 - Quantified Regulatory Exposure & Statutory
Liability
3.1) Truth in Lending Act / Regulation Z Civil Liability (15
U.S.C. §1640; 12 CFR §1026.13)
Legal anchor: Regulation Z (12 CFR §1026.13) imposes billing error investigation and correction
duties. The private right of action and civil liability framework is under the Truth in Lending Act
(15 U.S.C. §1640).
Recoverable categories in an individual consumer action may include:
● Actual damages (fact-based harm caused by mishandling)
● Statutory damages (subject to statutory limits in 15 U.S.C. §1640)
● Court costs
● Reasonable attorney’s fees (fee shifting upon prevailing)
3.1.A) Repeated Dispute Mishandling Increases Legal Exposure (Without
Overstating Caps)
This matter involves multiple discrete procedural failures, including:
18

● improper denial despite documented non-delivery,
● repeated “missing documentation” assertions despite submission,
● procedural closure tied to notice that was never received,
● appeal-level repetition of the same investigative defects.
While statutory damages in an individual action are capped under §1640, each discrete failure
can still support independent liability theories and increases:
● actual damages exposure,
● attorney-fee exposure, and
● the reasonableness of regulatory scrutiny when conduct continues after notice.
3.1.B) Conservative Civil Exposure Model (Defensible)
A conservative litigation exposure model typically includes:
● Statutory damages (capped by statute)
● Attorney’s fees (fee shifting): commonly $25,000-$75,000+ in contested
consumer-credit litigation (fact-dependent)
● Costs: $2,000-$10,000 (filing, service, transcripts)
Even conservatively, the fee-shifting reality alone can make the exposure materially exceed the
underlying principal dispute.
3.2) Restitution / Account Remediation Exposure
If the dispute is corrected on the merits, the creditor must unwind the transaction effects:
● Disputed principal: $10,500
● Accrued interest (variable)
● Any penalty or late fees triggered by the balance
● Credit reporting correction (if impacted)
This represents the clean remediation path.
3.3) Dodd-Frank UDAAP Civil Penalty Framework
(Regulatory context, not a consumer demand)
19

Under Dodd-Frank, regulators may impose civil money penalties for unfair or deceptive
practices. Current inflation-adjusted tiers:
- Tier 1: $7,217 per day
- Tier 2 (reckless): $36,083 per day
- Tier 3 (knowing): $1,443,275 per day
Example 10-day enforcement proxy:
Tier 1 → $72,170
Tier 2 → $360,830
Tier 3 → $14,432,750
This illustrates supervisory exposure environment, not a private claim.
3.3.A) Escalation Risk When Conduct Becomes Reckless
UDAAP tiers escalate based on mental state.
Once a creditor continues improper handling after notice, regulators evaluate conduct under
reckless or knowing tiers.
Patterns that trigger Tier 2 review include:
● repeated denial after notice
● ignoring documentary evidence
● procedural closure without investigation
Example 5-day Tier 2 window:
$36,083 × 5 = $180,415
This demonstrates why dispute handling is compliance-critical.
3.4) Merchant-Side Deceptive Practices Exposure
(Context)
If the merchant’s inducement claims and post-payment “term” assertions are proven deceptive,
the merchant may face liability under applicable state consumer protection and common-law
fraud principles.
This is included as context because it reinforces a core dispute fact: a merchant cannot retain
payment for an undelivered service by retroactively asserting unsigned terms, and the
20

bank should not treat unilateral, post-payment assertions as binding contractual terms where
there is no mutual assent.
3.5) Litigation Cost Reality Model
Even a modest consumer credit lawsuit commonly produces:
- Attorney fees: $25,000 - $100,000+
- Discovery / evidence handling: $5,000 - $25,000
- Depositions / expert review: $5,000 - $20,000
Total practical litigation environment: $35,000 - $145,000+ independent of the $10,500 principal.
3.6) Consolidated Conservative Exposure Summary
TILA/Reg Z statutory + fees: $47,000 - $105,000+
Restitution/remediation: $10,500 + interest/fees
Litigation cost environment: $35,000 - $145,000+
Regulatory penalty exposure: supervisory context only
3.7) Economic Rationality of Resolution
This dispute involves a principal amount of $10,500.
The modeled exposure environment conservatively exceeds: $50,000 – $150,000+
and escalates dramatically under supervisory review.
Correcting a documented services-not-rendered dispute is not a concession.
It is the lowest-risk economic outcome.

5.2) ECOA Non-Retaliation & Anti-Discrimination Notice
Federal law expressly prohibits retaliation or adverse action against a consumer for asserting
protected rights.
Under the Equal Credit Opportunity Act (ECOA), 15 U.S.C. §1691, and implementing
Regulation B:
● A creditor may not discriminate against or penalize a consumer
for exercising rights under consumer credit protection laws.
● Adverse treatment linked to dispute activity, complaints, or enforcement efforts is
prohibited.
Accordingly, I formally request and expect written confirmation that:
● This dispute and its resolution will not be reported or characterized negatively
● No adverse action, account limitation, repricing, or relationship impairment
will occur as a result of asserting my lawful dispute rights
● My standing and relationship with First Interstate Bank will remain intact
This request is consistent with ECOA, UDAAP principles, and CFPB supervisory expectations.

Accusatory)
Under Dodd-Frank’s UDAAP framework, conduct may be deemed unfair when it:
● causes substantial consumer injury,
● is not reasonably avoidable by the consumer,
● and is not outweighed by countervailing benefits.
Requiring a traveling consumer to respond to physical mail only,
- while refusing digital delivery,

- despite confirmed knowledge of travel status,

creates precisely the type of avoidable consumer harm UDAAP is designed to prevent.
This is especially true when the underlying transaction involves:
● documented non-delivery,
● partial refund admissions by the merchant,
● and reliance on unsigned post-payment terms.
I raise this not to accuse, but to underscore why prompt corrective action is the lowest-risk and
most compliant outcome.

listing, or revenue constituted non-delivery of the essential promised service?

representations and the absence of delivery?
M. Continued Enforcement After Knowledge
49. Yes or No: After receiving notice of non-delivery, unenforceable terms, and procedural
defects, did the bank take any corrective action before closing the dispute?
50. Yes or No: Did the bank escalate the dispute for supervisory or compliance review after
these issues were raised?
